# Changelog

## 2026-09-01 — Daily watch

**No new venue papers. Two new SiYuan advisories opened and read end-to-end from primary source, and together they turn the recurrence argument from a claim about *time* into a claim about *structure*.** Ledger moves **17 in-scope / 33 excluded / 14 context** (counts unchanged — both finds are new `key_advisories` inside the existing SiYuan context entry). Priority 3 from yesterday's list was executed; priorities 1 and 2 were attempted and are recorded as still open; 4 and 5 were correctly skipped as pre-notification.

### FIND 1 — CVE-2026-40922 / `GHSA-8q5w-mmxf-48jg`: a *third* incomplete fix, and a second XSS chain

Previously unknown to the ledger. Published **13 Apr 2026** by maintainer `88250`, **Moderate**, CWE-79, affected `< b382f50e1880`, patched **v3.6.4**. Title, verbatim: *"Incomplete fix for CVE-2026-33066."*

The mechanism is what makes it valuable. -33066's fix added `luteEngine.SetSanitize(true)`. That sanitizer, in `lute/render/sanitizer.go`, has two combining defects: `<iframe>` **is explicitly commented out** of `setOfElementsToSkipContent` (the advisory's own PoC reproduces the Go set and annotates it `# NOTE: iframe is commented out in the original Go code!`), and `srcdoc` is classified as a URL attribute and tested against scheme prefixes (`javascript:`, `data:text/html`) even though *"`srcdoc` contains raw HTML content, not a URL."* So `<iframe srcdoc="<script>alert(document.domain)</script>">` survives sanitization and executes in a nested browsing context.

**New escalation primitive (ix): sanitizer allowlist gap via nested browsing context** — an attribute-model *type confusion inside the sanitizer itself*. This is categorically distinct from (vi) sanitizer/decode-path mismatch and (vii) encoder alphabet mismatch: here the check is well-implemented and simply irrelevant to the value it inspects. Any analysis that treats `SetSanitize(true)` as a sound barrier marks this clean — the same trust failure as -50551's `escapeAttr`, but one level up, at the library rather than the call site.

**Why this is the run's headline.** The app now has **two independent XSS incomplete-fix chains** (plus the known path-traversal one), and Chain B's failure mode differs from Chain A's. Taken together the three enumerate how sink-level remediation fails: **(i) the sanitizer is never called** (-33066); **(ii) the correct sanitizer is called at some sibling sites and not others** (-50551, -44588, -33067); **(iii) the sanitizer is called correctly everywhere and is itself inadequate** (-40922). That is a *closed taxonomy of sink-patching failure observed in one application within one year* — materially stronger than "fixes are incomplete," and the direct empirical answer to the objection that hardening sinks substitutes for auditing the profile.

**Recorded, not resolved:** the advisory asserts *"arbitrary code execution on the user's machine"* yet is scored **Moderate with no CVSS vector**, while its own parent -33066 — same surface, same delivery, same Electron context — is **Critical 9.6**. That is a **seventh** severity inconsistency, and the first one *internal to a single chain*: a CVSS-keyed aggregation would rank the incomplete fix below the bug it failed to fix. Also flagged: -33066 located the defect in `renderREADME()` at `kernel/bazaar/package.go:635-645`, this one in `renderPackageREADME()` in `kernel/bazaar/readme.go`. Rename or sibling function — unresolved until the `b382f50` diff is read.

### FIND 2 — CVE-2026-33067 / `GHSA-mvpm-v6q4-m2pf`: priority 3 closed, and the meta-pattern tightens to two lines

Read end-to-end. **Critical 9.6**, `AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:H` — the corpus's **second `UI:N`** advisory and the first to reach zero-click through a *supply-chain* channel rather than sync. Published 17 Mar 2026, reporter **0xkakash1**, affected `<= 3.5.9`, patched **v3.6.1**. Sink `app/src/config/bazaar.ts:275-277`; profile `app/electron/main.js:422-426`.

The escalation primitive is **(viii) non-uniform sanitizer application — third independent instance, and the tightest yet.** Not across languages (as in -33066) or across sibling renderers (as in -50551), but across **two expressions ~2 lines apart in one HTML template**: the `title` *attribute* of the description div is escaped with `escapeAttr()`, the *text content* of that same div, carrying the same variable, is not. Advisory verbatim: *"the risk was partially recognized but incompletely mitigated."* The meta-pattern now holds at three granularities — across languages, across files, and inside a single template literal. **A checker that flags divergent sanitizer choice on one variable within one render function needs no taint analysis, no sink semantics and no Electron model, and catches this instance.**

Zero-click is stated plainly: a `plugin.json` `displayName` of `Helpful Plugin<img src=x onerror="require('child_process').exec('calc.exe')">`, submitted through the normal Bazaar PR process, fires when *"any SiYuan desktop user navigates to Settings > Bazaar > Plugins"* — *"Browsing the marketplace is sufficient."* Escalations given in full: reverse shell, and API-token theft reading `~/.config/siyuan/cookie.key`. The recommended fix includes something new for this corpus: **server-side sanitization in the Bazaar index build pipeline**, with a concrete Go `sanitizePackageDisplayStrings()` — the first advisory here to propose fixing an Electron client-side XSS at the *package-index* level, i.e. to treat the marketplace as the trust boundary.

### The structural finding: five advisories on one surface, recurrence across a *field space*

The Bazaar marketplace alone now accounts for **five** advisories, four of them the same defect rediscovered in **different fields of the same struct**: -33066 (README), -40922 (README, incomplete fix), -33067 (`displayName` + `description`), plus two new unopened leads **CVE-2026-45375** (`name` + `version` metadata) and **CVE-2026-54070** (README event handlers).

The repo's existing framing is recurrence over *time*. This is recurrence over the **field space of one data structure, at essentially one moment** — -33066 and -33067 were published the same day, by the same reporter, split from the same parent report `GHSA-v3mg-9v85-fcm7`. `displayName`, `description`, `name` and `version` are siblings in one `plugin.json`, all attacker-controlled, all reaching one render path, and they were fixed one or two at a time across five months. **The thesis-usable claim: a discovery method that enumerates every field of an externally-supplied data structure and checks each field's render path finds all four in one pass; the per-report, per-field process that actually occurred produced four advisories.** That is a measurable argument for structure-aware discovery over sink-by-sink reporting, grounded entirely in vendor records. Caveat: -45375 and -54070 are known only from GLAD search-result titles — versions, scores and sinks **unverified, do not quote**.

SiYuan's count moves **twelve-plus → fourteen-plus**, still a floor.

## 2026-08-31 — Daily watch

**No new venue papers, but the run closed its top-priority open question and turned the SiYuan case study into something structurally different.** Ledger moves **17 in-scope / 32 → 33 excluded / 13 → 14 context**. Three of the four items on yesterday's priority list were executed; the fourth and fifth were correctly skipped as pre-notification.

### PRIORITY 1 — `seclab-taskflows` opened. The answer is favourable to the thesis.

Yesterday's entry called this "the highest-value action available by a wide margin," because the Vikunja CVE is the corpus's only tool-attributed discovery and it was unknown whether the tool was general or Electron-specific. **It is general, and the evidence is stronger than a null result.**

Two files were read in full from primary source: the repo README, and `src/seclab_taskflows/taskflows/audit/classify_application_local.yaml` (88 lines, at commit `4d8c3db`). What they show:

- **No Electron model of any kind.** Nothing in the README or the triage taskflow names Electron, `nodeIntegration`, `contextIsolation`, `preload` or `contextBridge`. The framework is an agentic repo auditor built around a CodeQL MCP server, run whole-repository (documented demo targets: `github/cmark-gfm`, OWASP Juice Shop), writing to an SQLite `audit_result` table with a `has_vulnerability` column. 76 stars, 22 forks, 282 commits, MIT.
- **And then the genuinely interesting part.** The triage prompt instructs the model **twice, verbatim**, to exclude exactly the finding class the Vikunja CVE consists of: *"take care not to include issues that are of low severity or requires unrealistic attack scenario such as misconfiguration"* and *"do not include issues that requires system to be already compromised, such as issues that relies on maliciious [sic] configurations, stolen credentials, misconfiguration etc."*

So the one tool credited with landing this bug class ships a prompt telling the agent that misconfigurations are not reportable. **That is a citable piece of evidence for the thesis's own framing**: generic auditing pipelines systematically deprioritise the Electron config class, which is a reason the class keeps recurring in the advisory record. Prior art is bounded, and an Electron-specialised discovery method is not pre-empted.

Caveats recorded in the ledger and worth repeating: this is one taskflow of many, read at a pinned commit rather than `main`; the `audit/` directory could not be enumerated (GitHub tree views are React-rendered and return an empty body to `web_fetch` — same failure mode as the RAID '26 SPA); and it is **not** established that the Vikunja find came from this particular taskflow.

### PRIORITY 2 — all three named SiYuan advisories opened, and they produce a three-link incomplete-fix chain

**The headline finding of the run.** The aria-label sink family is now a documented chain of *three* CVEs and *two* successive failed remediations, each link named by the next one's own advisory:

**CVE-2026-34585 → (incompletely fixed by) CVE-2026-44588 → (whose `escapeAriaLabel` fix was applied non-uniformly, producing) CVE-2026-50551.**

This is stronger than the repo's prior framing. "Fixed at the sink, never at the profile" said sink-patching does not close the *class*. The chain says sink-patching **does not reliably hold even at the sink** — which is the empirical answer to the obvious objection that hardening sinks is a sufficient alternative to auditing the profile. A second, independent chain exists on a different weakness class (`GHSA-gw25-m53r-qh88`, path traversal, incompletely fixing `GHSA-6865-qjcf-286f`).

Per advisory:

- **CVE-2026-44588 / `GHSA-25rp-h46x-2hjm`** — 9.6 Critical, `AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:H` (**PR:N**). CWE-116 + **CWE-1188** + CWE-79. New escalation primitive **(vii) encoder/decoder alphabet mismatch**: `escapeAriaLabel` (`app/src/util/escape.ts:19-25`) handles HTML special characters but leaves `%XX` untouched, so `%3Cimg …%3E` round-trips intact until `decodeURIComponent` on the consumer side turns `%3C` into a **literal** `<` and `innerHTML` tokenizes it. Distinct from -50551's (vi): there a correct escape is undone by entity decoding; here the escaper never modelled the alphabet at all. **Two facts worth flagging.** First, CWE-1188 "Initialization of a Resource with an Insecure Default" — the profile itself getting a weakness class inside an advisory that *also* has a concrete sink, i.e. the (A2) config-alone argument appearing inside an (A1) finding. Second, the config here is cited at **`main.js:407-411`** and includes **`webSecurity: false`**, while -50551 cites `main.js:307` and -33066 cites `main.js:422-426` — same profile, three line ranges, three versions. **Line-anchored detection of this profile is fragile; pattern-anchored detection is not.**
- **CVE-2026-33066 / `GHSA-4663-4mpg-879v`** — 9.6 Critical, `PR:L`, published 17 Mar 2026, affected `<= 3.5.9`, patched `v3.6.1`, reporter **0xkakash1** (a *fourth* independent reporter). New escalation primitive **(viii) non-uniform application of a sanitizer across sibling call sites**: `renderREADME` (`kernel/bazaar/package.go:635-645`) calls `lute.New()` **without** `SetSanitize(true)`, while the note renderer at `kernel/util/lute.go:81` **does** — and the advisory says so explicitly: *"This inconsistency demonstrates that the project is aware of the Lute sanitization API but failed to apply it to Bazaar content."* **This is the most tool-actionable finding the watch has produced.** It is detectable by enumerating every construction site of a shared renderer/escaper and flagging configuration divergence — no sink semantics, no taint flow, no Electron model required. And it is structurally identical to the `escapeAriaLabel`-vs-`escapeAttr` divergence in -50551: **the same meta-pattern, twice, in one application, four months apart, across two languages.** Also a **new delivery channel**: the official SiYuan **Bazaar marketplace**, the first curated first-party package channel in the corpus, which links this application thread to the config's supply-chain include category without involving npm.
- **CVE-2026-29183 / `GHSA-6865-qjcf-286f`** — 9.3 Critical, `AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:C/C:H/I:H/**A:N**`, patched 3.5.9. Unauthenticated reflected SVG XSS in `/api/icon/getDynamicIcon` (`type=8`). **It does not claim RCE.** Same app, same CWE, same year, and the vendor stops at JavaScript execution plus authenticated API abuse. That makes it the **within-application control case**: this vendor *does* distinguish XSS-only from XSS→RCE when scoring, so the 9.9/9.6/9.0 figures elsewhere are not blanket inflation. It also adds SiYuan's **third distinct source surface** — stored content, the `siyuan://` deep link, and now the app's own unauthenticated local HTTP API — a direct argument that a tool scoped only to renderer DOM sinks, or only to IPC, under-covers one real application.

Taxonomy updated to **`cross_app_note_v5`**: 17+ advisories / 5 apps, a new root-cause family **(C) XSS with no asserted escalation**, eight escalation primitives, and a **six-way** severity-scoring inconsistency. SiYuan's own count is now **twelve-plus** and should be read as a floor — five further advisories were named this run and left unopened (`CVE-2026-34585`, `GHSA-v3mg-9v85-fcm7`, `GHSA-mvpm-v6q4-m2pf`, `GHSA-w3gq-5j72-36vc`, `GHSA-gw25-m53r-qh88`).

**One unresolved discrepancy, recorded rather than papered over.** GLAD reports *"there is no solution available yet"* for CVE-2026-44588, while CVE-2026-50551's advisory says a fix for -44588 shipped and used `escapeAriaLabel()`. Both cannot be current. Most likely GLAD's gomod record lags the app-side fix, but that is unverified — **do not state a patched version for -44588** until `GHSA-25rp-h46x-2hjm` itself is read. Grounding is also uneven across the three: -33066 was read end-to-end from its GHSA page, -44588 in full from GLAD (GHSA and NVD not opened), and **-29183 from a search summary only** — every figure for it is provisional, and the "does not claim RCE" observation is an argument from absence in a summary, which is exactly what a summary can drop.

## 2026-08-30 — Daily watch

**No new venue papers — but two substantive advisory finds, both read end-to-end from primary sources, and one of them closes an explicitly-flagged open lead.** Ledger moves **17 in-scope / 32 excluded / 12 → 13 context**. All nine venues checked or accounted for; nothing new at any of them.

The productive channel today was, once again, **not** the venue sweep. Both finds came from the standing `XSS to RCE cross-platform desktop app` query, which has now produced material on four separate runs. The venue-targeted phrasings produced nothing, as they have for seven consecutive runs.

### FIND 1 — SiYuan CVE-2026-50551 (GHSA-56mp-4f3v-fgj2): closes an open lead, and upgrades the argument

The 08-13 grounding note listed four unopened SiYuan CVE leads with the instruction *"do not quote them until checked against their own advisories."* **CVE-2026-50551 is now opened, read in full, and recorded** — it may be quoted. The other three (CVE-2026-54759, CVE-2026-54067, CVE-2026-55570) remain closed leads.

Headline facts: *Stored XSS to RCE via Unsanitized Attribute View Asset Cell Content*, **Critical 9.9**, `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:H`, published 3 Jun 2026 by maintainer `88250`, affected `<= v3.6.5`, patched `v3.7.0`, CWE-79, reporter **Yunkaiwjs** (a *third* independent reporter against SiYuan). Renderer profile unchanged as ever: `nodeIntegration: true`, `contextIsolation: false` at `app/electron/main.js:307`.

**Three reasons this is more than a ninth tally mark.**

1. **It names the mechanism behind the recurrence.** Prior runs could only assert *"fixed at the sink, never at the profile."* This advisory says why, in the vendor's words: the fix for **CVE-2026-44588** used `escapeAriaLabel()` (double-escapes `<`) but the Attribute-View asset renderers were left on the weaker `escapeAttr()` (quotes only) — *"part of a pattern of incomplete fixes in SiYuan (see also CVE-2026-33066, CVE-2026-29183)."* The failure is **non-uniform application of a correct fix across sibling renderers sharing one data type** — which is a property a discovery tool can be built to detect.

2. **New escalation primitive (vi): sanitizer/decode-path mismatch.** Sink 2 is *not* an unsanitized flow. `escapeAttr()` is applied and is correct for writing an HTML attribute. The defect is the round-trip: attribute → `getAttribute()` (which entity-**decodes**) → `decodeURIComponent` → `innerHTML`, at which point `<` and `>` are live again. **Any analysis that treats `escapeAttr` as a sanitizer marks this flow clean.** This is the corpus's first sink whose defect is a *mismatch* between sanitizer and downstream decode path rather than a *missing* sanitizer — a concrete, citable gap in sanitizer-aware static analysis.

3. **First `UI:N` advisory in the corpus, and the highest score in it.** Sink 1 fires on page load; opening a synced note suffices. Everything else here is `UI:R`. Plus sync propagation to all users and persistence in `.sy` files.

**Three new leads harvested from its text** — `CVE-2026-44588` / `GHSA-25rp-h46x-2hjm`, `CVE-2026-33066` / `GHSA-4663-4mpg-879v`, `CVE-2026-29183` / `GHSA-6865-qjcf-286f`. **None appears in the seven-item list inside `GHSA-6gx2-8gcr-x83f`**, so the SiYuan recurrence set is materially larger than the eight this repo had counted. The entry title now reads **"nine-plus"** and should be treated as a floor.

### FIND 2 — Vikunja CVE-2026-33334 (GHSA-xh67-63q3-hf7g): a CVE for the *config alone*

Fifth independent application, and the most argument-relevant advisory the watch has surfaced — **because of what it does not contain.** No source, no sink, no XSS, no payload. The vendor assigned a CVE to the `webPreferences` profile itself, arguing that *"any cross-site scripting (XSS) vulnerability in the Vikunja web frontend — present or future — automatically escalates to full remote code execution."* Published 20 Mar 2026 by `kolaente`, npm `vikunja-desktop`, affected `>= 0.21.0`, patched `2.2.0`, **CWE-94 + CWE-269**. Root cause is a seven-line constructor at `desktop/main.js:11-17` whose entire content is `webPreferences: { nodeIntegration: true }`.

**Why it matters.** There is a standing objection to the Inspectron / config-auditing line: an unsafe config is a *risk multiplier*, not a bug, so config findings aren't vulnerabilities until someone also finds an XSS. **Here is a vendor, with a CNA-issued CVE rated High, publicly disagreeing.** A thesis building a config-auditing discovery tool can cite this as evidence that such a tool's raw output is itself reportable — which answers the "so what" question about Inspectron-style results directly. Family (A) accordingly now splits into **(A1) config + concrete sink** (the normal case) and **(A2) config alone** (Vikunja) — and A2 is exactly what a config auditor emits.

**⚠️ The single most important line on the page, and the top follow-up for the next run:**

> *"This vulnerability was found using GitHub Security Lab Taskflows"* — `github.com/GitHubSecurityLab/seclab-taskflows`

**This is the first tool-attributed discovery in the entire corpus.** Every other advisory is credited to a named human. ## 2026-08-29 — Daily watch

**No new papers today.** Ledger unchanged at **17 in-scope / 32 excluded / 12 context**; only `last_updated` / `last_run` moved to 2026-08-29. But this run produced **two things prior runs did not**: (1) the **root cause of the 17-run RAID '26 "empty page" mystery**, which was a wrong diagnosis all along, and (2) **first-party grounding for the LLMVD.js numbers** that the 08-28 entry explicitly flagged as ungrounded.

### RAID '26 — SOLVED: the page is not empty, it is JavaScript-rendered. Retract the "withheld" theory.

Every run since mid-July has fetched `https://raid2026.org/accepted.html`, seen a bare `## Accepted papers` heading, and concluded the organisers were sitting on the list. **That conclusion was wrong, or at least unfounded.** This run fetched the website's source repo — `https://github.com/sptagelab/RAID2026` — and its README states the architecture outright:

- `js/data-loader.js` — *"CSV parsing and data loading / Accepted papers rendering / Search functionality / Separate `DOMContentLoaded` listener for accepted papers page only"*
- `data/test_accepted_papers.csv` — the backing data file, format `id,title,authors` with `;`-separated authors
- README §"Adding/Updating Accepted Papers" instructs organisers to **edit the CSV**, and notes *"The browser will automatically load new data the next day."*

So `accepted.html` is a **client-side-rendered shell**. `web_fetch` does not execute JavaScript, therefore it **can never** show paper titles no matter how many times it is retried — the 17 "empty" fetches carry **zero information** about whether the list exists. The 08-27 speculation about the list "being withheld rather than forgotten," and the 08-28 note that the site "IS being actively edited" but hasn't "shipped the paper list," are both **retracted**: we simply have not been reading the right resource.

**The real channel is the CSV.** Two ways to reach it:

1. `https://raid2026.org/data/test_accepted_papers.csv` (served by the conference site)
2. `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sptagelab/RAID2026/main/data/test_accepted_papers.csv` (raw from the source repo)

**Neither could be fetched this run**, for a tooling reason worth recording precisely so the next run does not waste attempts: `web_fetch` enforces a **provenance rule** — it will only retrieve a URL that appeared as a *link* in a prior fetch result, a search result, or a user message. The CSV path appears in the GitHub README only as **inline code text**, not as a hyperlink, so it never entered the provenance set. Fetching `.../tree/main/data` and `.../commits/main` both returned **empty**, because GitHub's own file-browser and commit views are React-rendered — the same JS problem one level up. The in-app browser (which *would* render the JS) refused navigation to both `raid2026.org` and `raw.githubusercontent.com`: site approval cannot be granted in an unattended scheduled run.

**Action for the next run, in priority order:**

- **Ask the user to paste either CSV URL verbatim into a message once.** That single action puts it in the provenance set and unblocks the whole channel permanently. This is the cheapest fix by a wide margin and should be requested explicitly.
- Alternatively, run the watch **interactively once** and approve `raid2026.org` in the browser pane, then read the rendered `accepted.html`.
- **Stop counting "empty fetches" of `accepted.html` as evidence.** It is a shell. Retire the streak counter.
- The 08-28 finding that `program.html` is a `TBD` template stub still stands and is unaffected — though note it is *also* template-loaded, so it deserves the same skepticism if it ever appears to have content.

**Generalised lesson for this watch:** at least one venue in the config is now a single-page app. Before concluding "a venue has published nothing," check whether the page is client-rendered — a giveaway is a heading with nothing under it plus a `<script>`-driven `includes/` or `data/` directory. Worth applying to any future venue page that looks suspiciously bare.

### LLMVD.js (arXiv:2604.20179) — numbers now grounded in the PDF, not a search summary

The 08-28 entry recorded LLMVD.js's headline comparisons but flagged them as **"NOT read from the PDF"** because the abs fetch returned an empty PDF. This run the abs page failed the same way (`Content-Type: application/pdf`, no machine-readable text) but **`https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.20179` fetched successfully**, and the abstract was read directly. All four figures **confirm** the 08-28 record, quoted from the paper's own abstract:

> *"For packages from public benchmarks, LLMVD.js confirms **84%** of the vulnerabilities, compared to **less than 22%** for prior program analysis tools. … When evaluated on a set of **260** recently released packages (without vulnerability groundtruth information), traditional tools produce validated exploits for few (**≤ 2**) packages, while LLMVD.js generates validated exploits for **36** packages."*

Contribution list also confirms **"36 previously undocumented vulnerabilities in recently released Node.js packages,"** all reported to maintainers. Datasets are four: two public benchmarks (**VulcaN** and one other), a private real-world dataset, a memorization-robustness transformed variant, and the 260 new packages.

**Status unchanged: still deliberately NOT ledgered** — not a watched venue, not Electron-specific, unrefereed preprint. It remains a **promotion watch** alongside Buzz to Boom. **The 08-28 caveat "these numbers are currently ungrounded" can now be struck.**

**Recipe worth reusing:** when an arXiv `abs` page returns an empty PDF, fetch `arxiv.org/pdf/<id>` instead — it returned 100k characters of clean text where `abs` returned nothing.

**Taint-Style Vulnerability Detection and Confirmation for Node.js Packages Using LLM Agent Reasoning** — Ronghao Ni, Mihai Christodorescu, **Limin Jia**; arXiv **2604.20179**, submitted **22 Apr 2026**. Surfaced by a rotated standing query, not by a ToC sweep.

### CCS '26 accepted-papers page recovered — and the fix is the 08-24 workaround, confirmed working

The 08-24 and 08-25 runs both recorded the same failure: `https://www.sigsac.org/ccs/CCS2026/program/accepted-papers.html` could not be fetched because **no search surfaced the URL, so provenance was never seeded**. Today reproduced that failure once (a plain `CCS 2026 accepted papers second cycle sigsac` search returned only CFP pages, HotCRP instances, and the CCS **2025** list) and then broke it with a **quoted-fragment search**: `"CCS2026" "accepted-papers" sigsac.org program`. That returned the exact URL as its fifth result, provenance was seeded, and the fetch succeeded — **81,704 chars, 222 lines, ~193 paper rows**.

**Record this as the standing recipe for sigsac.org: search the URL's own path fragments in quotes, not the human-readable page name.** Searching for *the concept* ("CCS 2026 accepted papers") returns the CFP and the previous year's list; searching for *the string* (`"CCS2026" "accepted-papers"`) returns the page itself. This is cheaper and more reliable than the 08-24 suggestion of searching for a known paper title, and it should generalize to any venue whose accepted-papers page is a static file under a predictable path.

### The fetch that worked — `sec26_contents.pdf`. Record this recipe; it beats the technical-sessions page outright.

Yesterday's run left a clear instruction: *"Next run's priority #1: USENIX Sec '26 Cycle 2 — the symposium has now passed."* Executing it exposed a structural problem and then a better channel.

- **There is no Cycle 2 accepted-papers page.** USENIX published `cycle1-accepted-papers` but never a `cycle2-` equivalent; Cycle 2 papers were folded straight into the program. Grepping the site nav confirms only two schedule children exist: `technical-sessions` and `cycle1-accepted-papers`. **Stop looking for a Cycle 2 page — it does not exist.**
- **`technical-sessions` is unusable for a full sweep.** It renders as one giant page with day anchors (`#wedam`, `#thuam`, `#fripm`…), and `web_fetch` caps the render at ~122,745 chars — which lands **partway through Wednesday**. All 53 titles recovered were Wednesday-only, i.e. roughly a third of the symposium. The anchors are the *same URL*, so re-fetching `#thuam` just returns the dedup message. **Do not burn budget re-fetching this page by anchor.**
- **The fix: the proceedings front matter.** The technical-sessions render, truncated as it is, still contains the front-matter links near the top (line 109), including **`https://www.usenix.org/sites/default/files/sec26_contents.pdf`** — the **complete proceedings ToC**, every paper across both cycles, with authors and page numbers, in 87,122 chars. Verified complete: **Kintsugi at p. 731** (a Cycle 1 paper) and content running to **p. 4287**. One fetch, whole conference.

**Generalized lesson, worth applying to future venues: when a conference's HTML program is too large for `web_fetch`, look for the proceedings front-matter PDF (`*_contents.pdf`) linked from the top of that same page. It is far smaller, fully grep-able, and authoritative in a way an accepted-papers page is not.**

That is a **prototype-pollution-shaped break of contextBridge itself**, which is a tidy motivating example for the thesis (it sits exactly at the intersection of the ledger's prototype-pollution cluster and its Electron cluster). Industry-only, so **not ledgered** — but worth quoting in the introduction alongside CVE-2026-70599 and CVE-2026-34765 from the 08-24 run.

## 2026-08-23 — Daily watch

**No new in-scope papers. This run was a grounding run, and it paid off: the PDFuzzer full text is now read end-to-end, replacing yesterday's abstract-only note.** Ledger counts move to **17 in-scope / 30 excluded / 12 context** (the one new entry is an exclusion that closes an open lead).

### The fetch that worked — record the recipe, it is now proven twice

Yesterday's run ended on **HTTP 429** trying to fetch `arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06641`, and recorded that arXiv offers no HTML rendering for that submission. Today it fetched cleanly (**103,792 chars / 1,616 lines**) as fetch #6 of the run. The sequence that worked is the same corrected recipe the *Buzz to Boom* entry already carries:

1. **WebSearch the paper title.** This is the only thing that puts arXiv URLs into `web_fetch`'s provenance set. A cold `/pdf/` fetch is rejected outright.
2. The title search surfaced only `arxiv.org/abs/…`, **not** `/pdf/…` — so a direct `/pdf/` fetch still failed at this point.
3. **Fetch `arxiv.org/abs/…`.** Its `View PDF` link and `meta-citation_pdf_url` put the `/pdf/` URL into the provenance set.
4. **Then fetch `/pdf/…`.** Succeeded.

**Lesson to keep alongside the 08-22 CCS lesson: a 429 is a statement about that moment's budget, not about the document.** Do not convert a rate-limit into a permanent "unreachable" in the ledger.

### Rewritten — `papers/ccs2026-pdfuzzer.md`, now **full-text grounded**

The arXiv entry has moved to **v2 (18 Aug 2026)** and v2 is the **camera-ready**: it carries the **ACM DOI `10.1145/3830454.3832609`** and ISBN `979-8-4007-2871-6/2026/11` in its copyright block. That supersedes yesterday's "ACM DOI not yet assigned". Affiliations are now from the paper's own title block: seven UCSB authors, **Saad Ullah at Boston University**.

**What the full text changes about how this paper should be cited.**

- **"Up to 48%" is against Cooper specifically.** Against the strongest baseline (TypeOracle+Cooper) the gap is **19%**. Writing "48% better than state of the art" would misrepresent the paper. Full ladder: Cooper 48%, TypeOracle 37%, TO+Cooper 19%, TO+Favocado 17%, Favocado 15%, Claude-3.7-Sonnet 16–49%.
- **The 31 zero-days are a dedup count, not a raw crash count.** 57 crashing inputs (23 Adobe / 24 Foxit / 10 PDF-XChange) → statement-level reduction → dedup by normalized call-stack signature → **31 unique**. **11 potentially ACE. 26 of 31 fixed. 10 CVEs assigned.** The **$2,450 is from two bounties**, not from 26 — §1 and §6.2 word this differently and §6.2 is the precise one.
- **The number that actually matters for this thesis is 12 → 28.** Table 1's aggregated row: candidate-relationships-only finds **12** vulnerabilities; strong symbolic relationships find **28**; PDFuzzer-full **31**. Knowing which APIs *co-occur* is worth little; enforcing **parameter-value constraints and ordering** is what surfaces bugs.
- **Producer-consumer modelling is a bad prior.** Of 1,019 inferred strong relationships: **value-constraint 887 (87.0%)**, producer-consumer **69 (6.8%)**, implicit **63 (6.2%)**. Most prior API fuzzers model only the 6.8%.
- **Implicit relationships are the ones they solve worst — 64.0% instantiation success**, versus 95.1% value-constraint and 81.5% producer-consumer, because they need both a parameter constraint and a state precondition. **This is the single best hook for an Electron thesis:** state-mediated dependency (login → session handle → file access) is the *dominant* form of Electron IPC channel coupling, and it is precisely the form this paper handles least well.
- **§8 "Generalization Beyond PDF Readers" is a feasibility study only.** They extracted **2,925 Word VBA APIs / 3,768 parameters from 5,920 pages** of docs and built CFGs — but **ran no fuzzing and report no results**. Citing it as demonstrated generalization would be an overstatement.

Other now-grounded specifics recorded in the note: exact reader versions (Acrobat v24.005.20421, Foxit v2024.4.0.27683, PDF-XChange v10.5.2.395); **DynamoRIO** basic-block coverage over **five independent 24-hour runs** per tool per target; a **separate two-week** vulnerability campaign with `werfault.exe` crash detection and manual triage; Windows 8.1 VM with re-verification on Win10 22H2 / Win11 24H2; 666 API functions and 886 parameters; the 221,445-pair / 1,845-hour brute-force figure that motivates the two-stage RAG design; per-stage LLM accuracy 93/94/97/98%; and end-to-end cost $60.77 (GPT-4o) versus $76.14–$174.83 for the naive LLM baseline.

**One oddity recorded so it is not relitigated:** Table 4, Dimension 3, Foxit — `PC-Only` (397,916 bbks) scores **below** `None` (406,448), i.e. producer-consumer-only modelling looks *worse* than no relationship modelling at all on that target. The paper does not comment. Do not build an argument on the Dim-3 sub-rows.

**Still unread, and flagged at the top of the note:** Figure 2 is six coverage curves that flatten to axis labels only — no curve value may be quoted. **Table 1 is elided in the extracted text** (rows for IDs 10–14 and 26–28 collapse to `...`), so eight vulnerabilities' per-ID details are unavailable; the ten CVE identifiers listed in the note are those visible in un-elided rows, and the paper's own "10 assigned" is the authoritative count. If those rows are ever needed, try `arxiv.org/src/2608.06641` (TeX source).

### The unlock, because it generalises

`https://www.sigsac.org/ccs/CCS2026/program/accepted-papers.html` has probably existed for a little while — what changed is that a **search query phrased around the venue and city ("CCS 2026 accepted papers list November The Hague sigsac") surfaced it as a search-*result* link**, which is the only thing that puts a URL into `web_fetch`'s provenance set. Prior runs searched for "CCS 2026 accepted papers list sigsac" and got only the CFP, concluded the page did not exist, and never got a fetchable URL.

**Lesson to keep: "no search engine returns this URL" is evidence about the search query, not about the page.** When a venue's notification date has passed by more than a month, re-phrase the query (add the city, the month, the ordinal) before recording another "still unpublished".

### Added — `papers/ccs2026-pdfuzzer.md`

**From Documentation to Zero-day Vulnerabilities: LLM-Driven Fuzzing of JavaScript Engines in PDF Readers** — Suyue Guo, Stijn Pletinckx, Tianle Yu, Yigitcan Kaya, Saad Ullah, Wenbo Guo, Christopher Kruegel, Giovanni Vigna (UC Santa Barbara). Tool: **PDFuzzer**. **ACM CCS 2026, First Cycle**; preprint `arXiv:2608.06641` (6 Aug 2026, CC-BY 4.0, 16 pages), whose Comments field reads verbatim **"accepted by ACM CCS 2026"**. Tagged **ADJACENT**.

**Why it is in scope, in one line:** it is the same structural target as Electron — a native desktop application that embeds a JavaScript engine and hands script a large *documented* privileged host API — and it turns the vendor's own API manual into the grammar that drives discovery. An LLM mines context-free grammars and inter-call relationships from the readers' JavaScript API manuals plus execution traces; a constraint solver then instantiates concrete multi-call sequences. Against Adobe Acrobat Reader, Foxit PDF Reader and PDF-XChange Editor it reports **up to 48% higher coverage** than TypeOracle, Favocado, Cooper, Fuzz4All and a naive-LLM baseline, and **31 zero-days** ranging from information leakage to arbitrary code execution, with LLM stage accuracy **93–98%** and bug bounties received.

**Grounding caveat, recorded at the top of the note: the full text was NOT read.** `arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06641` returned **HTTP 429** and was not retried; arXiv provides **no HTML rendering** for this submission, so the cheap channel that works for *Buzz to Boom* is unavailable. Every number traces to the abstract. Do not write "31 CVEs" — the abstract says 31 *vulnerabilities* and does not give a CVE count. The word "Electron" never appears; the Electron mapping is this watch's analysis.

**The gap it leaves open, which is the useful part for a discovery thesis:** single-process engine memory safety only (no cross-process propagation), closed-source black-box targets (Electron source usually sits readable in the asar), and a single fixed input source (the document file) versus Electron's multi-source problem.

### Also excluded (five, all from the newly-readable CCS '26 list)

- **TANTRUM: Breaking the Heap Sandbox in JavaScript Engines via Protection Domain Fuzzing** — the closest call of the five, and the reasoning is recorded in the ledger so it is not relitigated. It attacks the **V8-style heap sandbox**, an engine-internal memory-safety mitigation reached only *after* the attacker already holds a corruption primitive. Contrast with **"Best of Both Worlds"** (S&P '26, in scope): that paper's subject is the **embedder bridge** — the host API a V8 embedder exposes to script, the direct analogue of `contextBridge`/preload — which an Electron app author actually controls. TANTRUM's subject is heap partitioning, which they do not. No preprint exists; only the title/author row was read.
- **Overloading Ad Blockers: Exploiting Filtering Logic for Selective Denial of Service** — browser-extension DoS; same rationale as DoubleX and KeyChaser.
- **Exploring Privacy Leakage and Data Disclosure Violations in the MacOS Application Ecosystem** — desktop apps, but privacy-compliance measurement rather than vulnerability discovery, and framework-agnostic.
- **Mini-Programs, Mega-Problems** (OAuth misuse in mini-programs) — mobile-only, same rationale as the already-excluded *Identity Confusion in WebView-based Mobile App-in-app Ecosystems*.
- **Reproducing Web Application Vulnerabilities with Patch-Guided Routing Inference and Sink Exploration** — pure server-side web; its sibling *BACAgent* is excluded for the same reason and is not separately ledgered.

### Correction to the two-step arXiv recipe (it failed at step 1 this run, for a new reason)

The 08-20/08-21 instruction was "fetch `arxiv.org/abs/2607.20698` first, then the PDF." Executed as fetch #1 of the run, **step 1 itself was rejected: "URL not in provenance set."** In a cold session *nothing* is in the provenance set until a WebSearch puts it there.

**Corrected recipe, which worked:** run a **WebSearch for the paper title first** — one search placed `arxiv.org/abs/…`, `arxiv.org/html/…` *and* `arxiv.org/pdf/…` in the provenance set together. ### Added — `papers/sp2026-site-isolation-dead.md`

**Site Isolation is Dead: How Site Isolation is Broken in Agentic Browsers and Extensions** — Suyoung Lee, Seongho Keum, Changoo Lee, Dongwon Shin (KAIST), Sanghyun Hong (Oregon State), Byoungyoung Lee (SNU), Sooel Son (KAIST). **IEEE S&P 2026**, DOI `10.1109/SP63933.2026.00241`, open-access author PDF at `wsp-lab.github.io/papers/lee-sp26.pdf`, artifact at `github.com/WSP-LAB/Site-Isolation-Is-Dead`. Tagged **ADJACENT**, note written from the **full text**.

**Why it is in scope, in one line:** it is the renderer→privileged-process IPC trust failure — the exact boundary this thesis targets — demonstrated end-to-end in real Chromium-based desktop applications. Nine systems analyzed (2 open-source agentic browsers + 7 Chrome extensions); **all nine vulnerable**; renderer RCE chained from CVE-2025-0291 + Chromium Issue 379140430 on Chromium 130, then **forged Mojo IPC at the C++ level bypasses site isolation with no additional sandbox escape**. Root causes named by the authors: no origin/authenticity check on IPC-borne prompts, and unrestricted renderer access to privileged storage. Their guardrail drops indirect-prompt-injection ASR **0.89 → 0.00** (BrowserOS) and **0.52 → 0.00** (Nanobrowser) for a 6-point TSR cost on 100 WebArena tasks.

**Caveat recorded prominently in the note: the paper never says "Electron"** (grep-confirmed across the full retrieved text). The Electron mapping — Chromium multi-process + privileged background process + IPC bridge ≈ renderer/main + `ipcRenderer`/`ipcMain`/`contextBridge` — is this watch's analysis, not the authors' claim.

**Method lesson worth keeping.** Prior runs swept the S&P '26 accepted-papers page and missed this, because the keyword grep (`electron|preload|contextIsolation|nodeIntegration|renderer|ipc|webview|npm|node.js`) does not match a title made of the words *site isolation*, *agentic browsers* and *extensions*. This run caught it only by widening the title screen to `browser|extension|desktop|isolation|javascript|v8|chromium` and then reading abstracts for the survivors. **Recommendation: keep the widened title screen; the config's keyword list is tuned for abstracts, not titles.**

## 2026-08-18 — Daily watch

**No new papers at any of the 9 venues, and no new advisory — but the single biggest grounding upgrade the watch has made to its most thesis-central item.** Ledger counts UNCHANGED at **15 in-scope / 22 excluded / 12 context**. No `papers/` note written; no `in_scope`, `excluded` or `context_non_venue` entry added.

**What changed: `arxiv.org/html/2607.20698` (*Buzz to Boom* / Proton) became readable and was read sequentially for the first time.** Every previous run had only the abstract plus a grepped intro. This run the full-text HTML (90,830 chars / 433 lines) was fetched and read start-to-finish through **§4.3** — abstract, §1 Introduction incl. the three-bullet Contributions, §2 (2.1 description, 2.2.1 process model, 2.2.2 Sources, 2.2.3 Sinks + the Table 1 caption), §3 Overview, §4.1 Segmentation Strategies, §4.2 Static Progression Analysis, §4.3 Segmented Fuzzing incl. Algorithm 1. **Hard limit: the retrieved HTML truncates mid-sentence inside §4.3 "Harness Synthesis" (line 433).** §4.4, §5 Implementation, **§6 Evaluation (all five RQs)**, §7–§9 and Appendices A–D are NOT in the retrieved text and remain unread.

**Five things this buys the thesis, all verbatim or near-verbatim from the paper.**

1. **The gap statement, in the authors' own words.** §1: *"existing Electron security studies mainly focus on unsafe configuration checks, and mitigation of malicious Document Object Model (DOM) content, or unsafe cross-context communication. None of them detects the progression of attacker-controlled messages across multiple Electron processes, let alone MPV."* It goes further on both sides: cross-context **static** analyses *"struggle with the dynamic message construction and asynchronous message passing"*, and existing **dynamic** web fuzzers *"are monolithic and do not coordinate across Electron's process boundaries"*, wasting budget on *"inputs that fail to propagate past the first boundary."* That is a published sentence naming the limitation of exactly the two lines this repo already holds in `in_scope` — Inspectron (config auditing) and NDSS'23 DOM-tree typing / COINDEF (DOM content).
2. **What makes an MPV different, stated crisply.** §1: *"A noteworthy property of a Message Progression Vulnerability … is the involvement of multiple Electron processes, leading to chaining two or more vulnerabilities through multi-step message passing"*, whereas prior message-related vulnerabilities *"only need at most one-step exploitation from one process to another."*
3. **A published source taxonomy that independently converges with this watch's advisory evidence.** §2.2.2 defines exactly three sources, coded U/F/S: **(U) Custom URI** (`app://action?content=payload`, delivered to the app's `open-url` handler by the OS), **(S) Remote Server Response** (attacker-controlled values reaching `fetch`; *"commonly arises when an intermediate vulnerability, such as a client-side request forgery (CSRF), happens"*, and *"commonly appears in applications supporting plugin installation, remote file loading, and MCP server imports"*), and **(F) Local File Access** — verbatim: *"No prior file system compromise is required: an attacker can exploit browser auto-download to silently place a malicious file in the default downloads directory, then trigger a custom URI to cause the application to load it."* Note the convergence: the twelve vendor advisories in this ledger produced delivery channels of shared content, clip sync, export, LLM output and **imported config file**; Proton arrives independently at **URI, server response, local file**. Both say the untrusted-input frontier of a desktop app is not the network request — the strongest available support for that thesis claim, now from two independent kinds of evidence.
4. **The utility process enters the attack surface — a first for this ledger.** The Paperlib motivating example (Figure 1 / §2.1) is a *"four-step chain, spanning three Electron processes"*: a malicious `paperlib://` URI clicked in a webpage → **unintended function invocation in the main process** → **client-side request forgery in the utility process** → **DOM-based XSS in the renderer** → arbitrary command execution. §2.2.1 confirms utility processes are *"spawned by the main to handle background tasks such as network requests and file access, and **also run with Node.js access**."* Every other item in the repo — advisories and papers alike — models Electron as renderer ↔ main only. (The CVE is redacted in the preprint as `CVE-2025-XXXX`.)
5. **A reusable vocabulary.** Intermediate vs. terminal sinks (*"intermediate sinks forward attacker-controlled data into a message destined for another process; terminal sinks directly cause the security consequence"*, and *"a single API may act as either depending on the runtime context"*); **Progression APIs**, which *"generalize the notion of message passing beyond explicit IPC"* to script execution, window navigation and custom protocol handlers; conditionally-terminal APIs named as `contents.executeJavaScript(T)` and `eval(T)`. A thesis can adopt this or explicitly argue against it, but it should not re-invent it.

**Recorded caveats — deliberately strict.** Because §6 was never retrieved, **every headline number still traces only to the abstract/intro** (589 apps, 23 zero-day MPVs, 22 to RCE, 13 acks / 11 fixes / 11 CVEs, +17 zero-days vs. end-to-end fuzzing). Per-RQ breakdowns, false-positive rates, runtime cost, the **LLM ablation (§6.5)** and the **segmented-vs-end-to-end table (§6.6)** are unverified and must not be quoted. Two claims that appeared in a search summary this run were checked against the retrieved text and **do NOT appear in it** — that the 589-app corpus spans *"January 2010 to April 2026"*, and that the agent uses *"claude-opus-4-7"*. Both are recorded as **UNCONFIRMED**; the model name is genuinely absent from §1–§4.3, and Appendix D ("Agent Design", D.1 Taint Analysis and Segmentation Agent / D.2 Harness Synthesis Agent) is exactly where it would live and was not retrieved.

**Promotion check — trigger NOT met (twelfth consecutive).** `arXiv:2607.20698` still a single **[v1] Wed, 22 Jul 2026 20:11:33 UTC (575 KB)**, Subjects **cs.CR** only, **no Comments / journal-ref / venue field**, DOI still arXiv-issued (DataCite pending), abstract byte-identical. The full-text read is a *grounding* upgrade, not a venue signal — no venue annotation appears anywhere in §1–§4.3 either.

## 2026-08-17 — Daily watch

**No new academic papers at any of the 9 venues — one new primary-source `context_non_venue` entry that turns the app-level evidence from "a pattern" into "a pattern one person can farm."** Ledger now **15 in-scope / 22 excluded / 12 context** (was 15/22/11). No `papers/` note written; no `in_scope` or `excluded` entry added.

**New `context_non_venue` entry: DbGate, `GHSA-35xm-qvjg-8m42` / CVE-2026-34725 — stored XSS→RCE through Svelte's `{@html}`.** Surfaced by the standing `XSS to RCE cross-platform desktop app` query and read **end-to-end from the GitHub advisory page**. DbGate (`dbgate/dbgate`) is a database-management GUI shipped both as a web app and as an Electron desktop app. **High 8.2**, `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:L/UI:R/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:H`, CWE-79 + CWE-94, EPSS 0.168%. npm `dbgate-web` **>= 7.0.0, < 7.1.5**, **patched 7.1.5** (fix commit `a7d2ed1`). Published in-repo **1 Apr 2026**, NVD 2 Apr, last updated 6 Apr. Publisher `Stelinkaa`, reporter **`ngocnn97`**.

The chain, verbatim from the advisory: `packages/web/src/icons/FontIcon.svelte` treats any icon string starting with `<svg` as inline SVG and renders it with **`{@html iconValue}` without sanitization**; `packages/api/src/controllers/apps.js` loads app definitions from disk and returns `applicationIcon` to clients **unchanged**; `DatabaseAppObject.svelte` passes it into `additionalIcons`; `AppObjectCore.svelte` renders it through `<FontIcon icon={ic.icon}>` — so `applicationIcon` is a stored XSS sink. `app/src/electron.js` sets **`nodeIntegration: true`, `contextIsolation: false`**. The PoC is an app-definition JSON whose icon is a benign `<svg>` followed by `<img src=x onerror="require('fs').writeFileSync(…,'xss-rce-poc.txt','poc')">`, with `usageRules` regexes set to `.*` so the icon is certain to render.

**Four things this buys the thesis, all defensible from the advisory text.**

1. **Same reporter, third app — the class is mechanically farmable.** `ngocnn97` is credited here, and (per this repo's own earlier entries) on **SiYuan** `GHSA-phhp-9rm9-6gr2` and **Notesnook** CVE-2026-33976. One researcher has landed the identical XSS→RCE technique against **three unrelated codebases**. That is the motivating observation a *discovery* thesis wants: if a human can replay it three times, the search is systematic enough to automate.
2. **A fifth escalation primitive, and a new kind of one.** Every prior sink in the corpus is a browser DOM API — `insertAdjacentHTML`, `contentDocument.write`, `srcdoc`, `innerHTML`. This one is **Svelte's `{@html}`**: a deliberate escape hatch out of a framework's automatic escaping. It generalizes to a compact, greppable predicate over the modern-frontend Electron population — `{@html}` / `v-html` / `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` reachable from stored data inside a `nodeIntegration:true` renderer — that a DOM-API-only sink list misses entirely.
3. **A new delivery channel: the app's own configuration file.** The payload arrives neither over the network nor by sync; the victim **imports or saves an attacker-supplied application-definition JSON**, which DbGate later loads via `apps/get-all-apps`. With Notesnook's clip-sync and export paths this supports a general claim — in desktop apps the untrusted-input frontier includes **project / config / profile files**, which a network-facing web fuzzer never reaches.
4. **Same bug, two severities — the cleanest motivation sentence in the corpus.** The advisory scores the *identical* Svelte code twice: on the web, "token theft, session hijacking, and performing privileged actions as the victim"; in Electron, local code execution. Identical source, identical sink; the desktop runtime alone converts a session bug into RCE. That is the thesis premise stated by a vendor, needing no interpretation.

**Corrected taxonomy (`cross_app_note_v3`, supersedes v2):** **twelve** vendor-published XSS→RCE advisories across **four** independent Electron apps (SiYuan ×8, Notesnook ×2, DeepChat ×1, DbGate ×1); **two** root-cause families — (A) unsafe `webPreferences` profile (SiYuan, Notesnook, **DbGate**), (B) over-broad IPC surface (DeepChat); **five** escalation primitives (direct DOM sink · same-origin unsandboxed iframe via `contentDocument.write` · same-origin unsandboxed iframe via `srcdoc` · renderer→main IPC into a spawn gadget · **framework raw-HTML directive**); **five** delivery channels (shared/network content · clip sync · export · LLM output/prompt injection · **imported config file**). Also newly flagged: a **severity-scoring inconsistency** — SiYuan/Notesnook/DeepChat are Critical 9.6 `AV:N`, DbGate is High 8.2 `AV:L`/`PR:L` for the same bug class. Any thesis that aggregates advisory CVSS across this corpus should say so.

**Recorded caveats.** The dbgate **repository source was not opened** — neither the `{@html}` sink nor the `electron.js` profile was independently confirmed, and **it was not checked whether 7.1.5 changed the profile or only the sink** (fix commit `a7d2ed1` unread). Given SiYuan and Notesnook, *"patched at the sink, profile unchanged"* is the **likely but unconfirmed** outcome — reading `a7d2ed1` is the obvious next step. The `ngocnn97` reporter-overlap claim rests on this repo's earlier entries, not a re-fetch of those advisories this run.

> **Lead noted, deliberately NOT ledgered: OPCConnect v2.34, CVE-2025-56459.** A stored XSS in the Taglist view of an Electron OT/SCADA engineering-station client, escalating via **`shell.openExternal('file:C:/Windows/System32/calc.exe')`** rather than `require()` — which would be a *sixth* escalation primitive, and the first in an industrial-control context. **No vendor advisory or NVD page was reached** this run; the only sources surfaced were a Medium write-up and a vendor blog. Grounding is insufficient for a ledger entry. Re-run `CVE-2025-56459 OPCConnect` next watch and ledger it only if a primary advisory is reachable.

## 2026-08-16 — Daily watch

**No new academic papers at any of the 9 venues — but a new primary-source `context_non_venue` entry that CORRECTS the generalization the last two runs were building toward.** Ledger now **15 in-scope / 22 excluded / 11 context** (was 15/22/10). No `papers/` note written; no `in_scope` or `excluded` entry added.

**New `context_non_venue` entry: DeepChat, `GHSA-v8v5-c872-mf8r` / CVE-2025-66222 — XSS→RCE through an over-broad IPC bridge.** Surfaced by the standing `XSS to RCE cross-platform desktop app` query and read **end-to-end from the GitHub advisory page**. DeepChat (`ThinkInAIXYZ/deepchat`, ~5.8k stars) is an LLM chat client; Critical **9.6**, CWE-94, published **3 Dec 2025**, affected `<=0.5.0`, **patched: None**, reporter `c2an1`.

The chain, verbatim from the advisory: `MermaidArtifact.vue` renders diagram content with `mermaidRef.value.innerHTML = props.block.content`, so an `<img onerror=…>` in Mermaid syntax fires **before** the Mermaid library sanitizes → the app hands the renderer **raw `ipcRenderer` as `window.electron`**, and the main process registers a **generic** router `ipcMain.handle('presenter:call', (event, name, method, ...payloads) => …)` allowing *any* method on *any* Presenter class → `McpPresenter.addMcpServer` accepts a config object with an **unvalidated `command`**, and `startServer` runs it via `child_process.spawn`. The PoC registers and starts `calc.exe` **under default settings — MCP does not even need to be enabled.**

**Why this changes the argument rather than just adding to it.**

1. **It breaks the profile-only story.** SiYuan (×8) and Notesnook (×2) both escalate through `nodeIntegration:true` + `contextIsolation:false` — an unsafe *`webPreferences` profile*. DeepChat's chain **does not depend on `nodeIntegration` at all**: it goes through a preload-exposed bridge. The corrected framing is **two root-cause families** — (A) unsafe profile, detectable by config auditing (Inspectron); (B) **over-broad IPC surface**, invisible to `webPreferences` inspection and requiring reasoning about what the IPC handlers actually reach.
2. **It is a real-world Message Progression Vulnerability.** renderer DOM sink → `presenter:call` IPC → main-process method → `child_process.spawn` is exactly the MPV class Proton / *Buzz to Boom* defines — field evidence from an app **outside** that paper's 589-app corpus.
3. **A delivery channel the watch has not seen: prompt injection.** Advisory, verbatim: *"Any malicious prompt injection (e.g., from a web search result, a shared conversation, or a malicious model response) can execute arbitrary system commands on the victim's machine."* The untrusted content enters via the **LLM's own output** — a surface neither config auditing, DOM-tree typing, nor network-facing fuzzing models.

**Corrected cross-application taxonomy** (supersedes the two-app note in the Notesnook entry; recorded as `cross_app_note_v2`): **eleven** vendor-published XSS→RCE advisories across **three** independent Electron apps, with **four** escalation primitives — (i) direct DOM sink in the top-level renderer (SiYuan `insertAdjacentHTML`), (ii) same-origin unsandboxed iframe via `contentDocument.write` (Notesnook clipper), (iii) same-origin unsandboxed iframe via `srcdoc` inheriting the parent origin (Notesnook export), (iv) **NEW** renderer→main IPC dispatch into a command-execution gadget (DeepChat). Only family (A) is visible from `webPreferences`.

**Recorded caveats.** The advisory **does not state DeepChat's `webPreferences`** — "does not depend on `nodeIntegration`" is an **inference** from the exploit mechanism (`window.electron` + `ipcRenderer.invoke`, i.e. a preload bridge, not renderer `require()`); **open the repo source before the thesis asserts `contextIsolation` was enabled**. Also unverified: whether a fix shipped after `0.5.0`; whether more DeepChat advisories exist (the repo nav shows "Security and quality 7"). Note the CVE year — **this advisory predates the SiYuan and Notesnook ones and was simply never surfaced by earlier runs**; it is new to the ledger, not new to the world.

## 2026-08-15 — Daily watch

**No new academic papers at any of the 9 venues — but a new primary-source `context_non_venue` entry that upgrades yesterday's app-level evidence from an anecdote to a cross-application pattern.** Ledger now **15 in-scope / 22 excluded / 10 context** (was 15/22/9). No `papers/` note written; no `in_scope` or `excluded` entry added.

**New `context_non_venue` entry: the Notesnook Electron XSS→RCE advisory pair, 2026.** Surfaced by the standing `XSS to RCE cross-platform desktop app` query, then read **end-to-end from both GitHub Security Advisory pages themselves**. Notesnook (`streetwriters/notesnook`, ~14k stars) is a note-taking app whose Electron desktop client is configured at `apps/desktop/src/main.ts:126` with `nodeIntegration: true`, `contextIsolation: false`, `nodeIntegrationInWorker: true` — and the vendor published **two Critical 9.6 XSS→RCE advisories against that same profile a month apart, through two entirely different feature paths, reported by two different people.**

- **`GHSA-f42f-phvp-43x5` / CVE-2026-33976** — *"RCE via stored XSS in Web Clipper rendering"*, published **25 Mar 2026**, affected Web/Desktop `<3.3.11` (patched 3.3.11) and iOS/Android `<3.3.17` (patched 3.3.17), reporter **`ngocnn97`** — *the same reporter as SiYuan's stored-XSS advisory `GHSA-phhp-9rm9-6gr2`*, i.e. one researcher replaying one technique across two unrelated Electron apps. The clipper's `toAttributes()` (`packages/clipper/src/index.ts`) copies **every** attribute off the source page's `documentElement` with no filtering, so `onload` / `onclick` / `onmouseover` survive into the stored `.clip`; the renderer (`packages/editor/src/extensions/web-clip/component.tsx`) then writes that HTML into a **same-origin, unsandboxed** iframe via `iframe.contentDocument.write(...)`. Payload propagates by **sync** and fires when the clip is opened on desktop.
- **`GHSA-fjm8-jg78-89h4` / CVE-2026-42090** — *"RCE via stored XSS in note export rendering"*, published **25 Apr 2026**, affected Web/Desktop `<3.3.15` (patched 3.3.15) and iOS/Android `<3.3.20` (patched 3.3.20), reporter **`iiihaiii`**. Note `title` / `headline` / `content` are interpolated into the export template **unescaped** (`packages/core/src/utils/templates/html/template.ts:31,33,210,211`), and `exportToPDF()` (`apps/web/src/common/export.ts:40`) sets **`iframe.srcdoc = content` with no `sandbox` attribute**. Advisory, verbatim: *"Because `srcdoc` inherits the parent origin, JavaScript running inside the exported iframe can call: `parent.require("fs")`."* PoC writes `%TEMP%\notesnook-xss-proof.txt` and separately spawns `calc.exe`.

**Why this is worth more than a second anecdote — four uses, all defensible from the advisory text.**

1. **Generality.** The SiYuan series alone could be dismissed as one badly-configured project. Notesnook is an independent codebase, maintainer and reporter exhibiting the identical escalation — enough to state the pattern as a **class**, not a case. The watch now holds **ten vendor-published XSS→RCE advisories across two independent Electron apps**, all against an unchanged `nodeIntegration:true` + `contextIsolation:false` profile.
2. **A second, distinct escalation primitive.** SiYuan escalates through a **direct DOM sink** in the top-level renderer (`insertAdjacentHTML`). Notesnook escalates through **same-origin unsandboxed iframes** — `contentDocument.write` (clipper) and `srcdoc` (export) — where the *missing `sandbox` attribute* is what carries Node privileges into attacker-controlled markup. That is a concrete, checkable predicate (`nodeIntegration && !contextIsolation && iframe without sandbox`) and, critically, **one that `webPreferences`-level auditing such as Inspectron would not by itself flag** — a clean gap argument against config-only analysis.
3. **The same per-sink-patching failure, independently reproduced.** Both fixes are at the sink (escape the template / filter the attributes); the `main.ts:126` profile is described identically in both advisories a month apart. This is the *"fixed at the sink, never at the profile"* dynamic that the SiYuan advisory names verbatim, now observed in a second project.
4. **Import/export and sync as attack surface.** Neither payload arrives over the network at render time — both ride the app's own data flows (clip sync, backup/import, PDF export). These are surfaces a network-facing fuzzer never reaches, and that Proton's message-passing model does not cover either.

**Recorded caveats.** The lead came from a TheHackerWire article and a search summary; **nothing from either is relied on** — every fact above is verbatim from the two GHSA pages. **Not verified:** whether Notesnook has since changed the `main.ts:126` profile (the repo source was not opened; the "unchanged" claim rests only on the April advisory still describing it identically to the March one — **checking current `main.ts` is the obvious next step**); whether further Notesnook advisories exist beyond this pair (no search was run); and note that GHSA-fjm8's Details/PoC section renders inside an unterminated code fence at the source, so its markdown structure is mangled although the substance was readable.

## 2026-08-13 — Daily watch

**No new academic papers at any of the 9 venues — but the run produced the best-grounded motivation material the watch has yet found, from a primary source.** Ledger now **15 in-scope / 22 excluded / 9 context** (was 15/22/8). No `papers/` note written; no `in_scope` or `excluded` entry added. Every calendar trigger this run resolved negative, which was expected — the informative part is the new context entry.

**New `context_non_venue` entry: the SiYuan Electron XSS→RCE advisory series, 2026.** Surfaced by the standing `XSS to RCE cross-platform desktop app` query, then read **end-to-end from both GitHub Security Advisory pages themselves** — this is a full primary-source entry, not a search-summary one. SiYuan (`siyuan-note/siyuan`, ~45k stars) is a personal knowledge-management app whose Electron desktop client ships an insecure renderer profile, and **the vendor has now published eight XSS-to-RCE advisories against that same profile without ever changing it.**

- **`GHSA-phhp-9rm9-6gr2` / CVE-2026-39846** (Critical **9.0**, `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:R/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:H`, CWE-79 + CWE-94, published **Apr 7 2026**, reporter `ngocnn97`, affected `<=v3.6.3`, patched `v3.6.4`). **Stored** XSS: the table `caption` attribute is saved into the node's IAL properties without escaping, then read back, HTML-**un**escaped, and written into the rendered DOM. Delivery is a crafted **`.sy.zip` note imported into a shared sync target** — the victim is compromised by syncing and opening the note. With `nodeIntegration: true` / `contextIsolation: false`, `<img src=x onerror=require('child_process').exec('calc')>` executes as OS code.
- **`GHSA-6gx2-8gcr-x83f`** (Critical **9.6**, `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:H`, CWE-79 + **CWE-1188 (insecure default)**, published **Jul 13 2026**, reporter Nguyen Van Hiep / `@hypnguyen1209` (MBBank), affected `3.7.1`, patched `v3.7.2`, **no CVE assigned**). **Reflected** XSS→OS RCE via the `siyuan://` deep link, with a file-and-line root-cause chain: `setAsDefaultProtocolClient("siyuan")` (`main.js:81,84`) → the URL is fanned out **with no allowlist or sanitizer** to *every* workspace window via `webContents.send("siyuan-open-url", url)` (`main.js:1692-1696`) → `ipcRenderer.on(SIYUAN_OPEN_URL, …)` (`onGetConfig.ts:168`) → `decodeURIComponent` of the path segment (`uri.ts:107`) → `openBazaarReadme` (`config/index.ts:88-134`) → `showMessage(\`Package not found: ${itemName}\`)` → **`insertAdjacentHTML`** (`message.ts:73,84`, plus sinks at 44-51 and 59).

**Why this is worth more than a motivating anecdote — three uses, all defensible from the advisory text.**

1. **The profile, stated by the vendor.** Every `BrowserWindow` runs `nodeIntegration:true, webviewTag:true, webSecurity:false, contextIsolation:false`, with `@electron/remote` global, `--disable-web-security` on the command line (`main.js:87`), and `content-security-policy` / `x-frame-options` / `access-control-allow-origin` **stripped from every response** (`main.js:554-565`). The advisory's own summary: *"There is no CSP, no SOP, no origin isolation."*
2. **The recurrence curve.** The advisory names **seven prior XSS-to-RCE advisories against the identical profile** (`GHSA-pcjq-j3mq-jv5j`, `-27qc-m5gf-jv5r`, `-mvjr-vv3c-w4qv`, `-ff66-236v-p4fg`, `-phhp-9rm9-6gr2`, `-grjj-6f6g-cq8q`, `-2h64-c999-c9r6`) and states, verbatim: ***"Each has been fixed at the sink, never at the profile."*** It adds that `showMessage` is the shared sink for **~20 sibling reflection paths**, so a per-call escape leaves the siblings live. That is direct empirical support — from the affected project, not from a paper's motivation section — for the claim that **per-sink patching does not close the class while the `webPreferences` profile is unsafe**, which is precisely the argument for tooling that audits the *profile* (Inspectron) or defends the *escalation* (COINDEF) instead of individual sinks.
3. **A field instance of message progression.** The deep-link path is *OS protocol handler → main process → unsanitized broadcast over renderer IPC → DOM sink → Node API*, i.e. a real, shipped, cross-process chain of exactly the shape **Proton / "Buzz to Boom"** defines as an MPV. It also composes: chaining `GHSA-cvhv-7xhj-xjp8` (unauthenticated workspace write) flips `Conf.Bazaar.Trust=true` on disk and removes the last non-default prerequisite, making the whole thing **zero-configuration and single-click**.

The advisory's recommended fix doubles as a clean statement of the secure baseline to measure real apps against: `contextIsolation:true, sandbox:true, nodeIntegration:false, webSecurity:true`, a `contextBridge` preload exposing only the required Node subset, and deletion of both `--disable-web-security` and the header-strip block. This entry is the **application-level counterpart** to the existing upstream-Electron CVE context entry, which covers the framework's own bugs — together they now cover both sides of the boundary.

**Recorded caveats.** The lead came from a **SentinelOne** vulnerability-database page that carries an explicit *"This content was generated using AI"* disclaimer and contradicts itself on the publication date (header "April 10" vs. its own timeline "April 7"); **nothing from it is relied on** — the GHSA date of Apr 7 is authoritative, and its CVSS 9.0 was confirmed against the advisory (a search summary claiming 9.1 was discarded). Four sibling SiYuan RCE CVEs it listed (**CVE-2026-54759, -50551, -54067, -55570**) were **not opened** and are recorded as leads only. The seven prior GHSA IDs were read from the advisory text but their own pages were not opened — **opening them would yield a fully dated eight-point recurrence timeline for a single app**, and that is the obvious next step whenever there is budget.

With the venue side quiet, the run's value came from the *other* channel entirely: a standing query that has been re-run for weeks without yield finally surfaced a vendor advisory whose text does the thesis's argumentative work better than a paper would — it documents eight repetitions of the target vulnerability class in one shipped app and says in the maintainers' own words why sink-level fixes do not close it. Worth generalizing: **the escalation-vocabulary queries (`XSS to RCE`, `desktop RCE`) are the ones paying off right now, while the venue-name queries are dormant.** Next real dates: **CCS '26 list (overdue)**, then **ACSAC '26 notification, Sept 8**. Scoped commit run via `scripts/commit_and_push.sh`.

2. **Direct grep of the USENIX Sec '26 program.** Both the Technical Sessions page **and — for the first time in this watch — the separate `/cycle1-accepted-papers` page** were fetched and grepped. A combined `electron|proton|message progression|Jianjia|Yinzhi` grep returns **zero occurrences** across either file.
3. **Two domain-restricted searches over `usenix.org`** for the Electron / nodeIntegration / contextBridge / preload / desktop vocabulary return only the **2024 Inspectron** paper.

The `promotion_trigger` in the ledger has been narrowed accordingly: USENIX Sec '26 is struck, and **CCS '26 is now the single most likely landing spot** — its accepted list is still unpublished **3.5+ weeks** after the Jul 17 notification, and **Cycle A camera-ready passed three days ago (Aug 9)**, with Cycle B camera-ready **Sept 13**.

**One grounding upgrade, and it corrects the record.** The **GLAD advisory page for CVE-2026-70610** ("contextBridge object copy honors prototype setters") was read in full — the last of the 08-11 open items that was reachable. Now confirmed from the advisory itself: **5.4 MEDIUM**, `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:H/PR:N/UI:N/S:C/C:L/I:L/A:N` (**note the scope change, S:C**), **CWE-1321**, published **Aug 5 2026**, affected `<39.8.9` / `40.0.0-alpha.1..<40.9.2` / `41.0.0-alpha.1..<41.2.2` / `42.0.0-alpha.1..<42.0.0-beta.4`. **Correction:** the 08-08/08-11 entries recorded a single fix, "PR #51083"; the advisory lists **four backport PRs (51083–51086) and four fix commits** (`17d5d264`, `23a6efb7`, `4ac50292`, `5b699544`), all now recorded so the C++ diff can be verified directly.

**The advisory's own impact text is the thesis-usable find, though.** Verbatim: *"Apps are only affected if their preload code accepts object arguments from untrusted content and reads properties from them without own-property checks. Apps that only accept primitive arguments, or that validate object arguments, are not affected."* That is the Electron maintainers stating, in one sentence, the precise preload coding pattern that converts prototype pollution into a context-isolation bypass — an **object-typed argument crossing `contextBridge`, read without `hasOwnProperty`/`Object.hasOwn`**. It is not a motivation quote; it is a **checkable detection predicate**, directly implementable as a static or dynamic rule over a shipped app's preload script, and it is exactly the kind of rule Inspectron (config auditing) and COINDEF (injection defense) do not express. It also sharpens the ledger's existing argument that CVE-2026-70610 is the missing link between the Node.js prototype-pollution line (Silent Spring, GHunter, Bullseye, UoP) and Electron proper.

**What is still not confirmed:** the `v8::Object::Set` → `CreateDataProperty` root-cause narrative, the `CreateProxyForAPI` / `electron_api_context_bridge.cc` attribution, and the "2026-04-16" fix date all still trace **only** to the AI-generated CVEReports write-up. The GitHub GHSA page appeared in GLAD's reference list but **could not be fetched** (blocked as out-of-provenance). With the four commit SHAs now in hand this is a one-step verification whenever GitHub is reachable.

The Cycle-2 embargo lifting did **not** change the page's fetchable extent. Its full-vocabulary grep returns **exactly one hit** — the already-excluded **PyGuard** cross-ecosystem NPM sentence. **`/cycle2-accepted-papers` was re-confirmed not to exist**, both by direct search and by its absence from the conference homepage nav. **Thu Aug 13 / Fri Aug 14 remain formally unverified** after a fifth route (`?page=1`) was blocked out-of-provenance; searches for Thursday/Friday session titles returned only conference-metadata sites.
