## 2026-08-11 — Daily watch

**No new in-scope papers — but two long-standing grounding gaps closed with primary sources, and one new exclusion.** Ledger now: **15 in-scope / 22 excluded / 8 context** (was 15/21/8). No `papers/` note written. This is the eve of the month's decisive date: **USENIX Sec '26 Cycle 2 lifts embargo tomorrow, Wed Aug 12**, with **RAID '26 camera-ready Thu Aug 13** right behind it.

- **"VCAligner: Aligning Source Distribution Versions with Upstream Git Commits to Secure Supply Chain"** (Li, Jiang, Zhou, Xu, Xie, Jia — Institute of Information Engineering, CAS; **DSN 2026**, Research Track) → **excluded**. Surfaced on this run's **end-to-end read of the full DSN '26 accepted-papers page** — Research + Industry + Disrupt + Doctoral Forum + Poster tracks, no truncation — which is the first time that page has been swept completely; earlier passes had recorded only *Demystifying PWA Permission Systems* and *VulJSFormer* from it. VCAligner attacks the **"Provenance Gap"** between rigorously reviewed upstream source and loosely scrutinized released artifacts (the 2024 XZ Utils backdoor is the motivating case), building content-based inverted indexes over VCS history to map a released distribution back to its originating commit without relying on fragile version tags. On **2,984 verifiable PyPI packages** (from the 4,000 most-downloaded projects with public GitHub upstreams), tag-based heuristics work for 85% but the residual 15% implies **>10.3M commits of audit workload**, which VCAligner cuts **~158×** to **<65,000**. Matched only generic "supply chain" vocabulary; PyPI/Python + Git tarballs throughout, no npm / Node.js runtime / Electron angle → out of scope, same rationale as ACSAC'23 npm/PyPI, NDSS'26 *From Noise to Signal*, AsiaCCS'26 *Original Sin of npm*, USENIX Sec'26 PyGuard, ESORICS'26 *Short Paths, Real Risks*. Numbers are **search-summary grounded** (IEEE Xplore page not opened) and flagged as such in the ledger.

**Two open items closed, both with primary or near-primary sources — this is the run's real yield.**

1. **CVE-2026-70598 is now grounded and ledgered.** The 08-10 run surfaced it from a third-party aggregator headline and *correctly refused* to record it. The **GLAD advisory page has now been read in full**: *"Off-screen rendering trusts GPU-supplied geometry over shared-memory size"*, published **2026-08-05**, **3.9 LOW**, `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:H/PR:H/UI:N/S:C/C:L/I:N/A:L`, **CWE-125 (out-of-bounds read)**, affected `<39.8.10` / `40.0.0-alpha.1..<40.9.0` / `41.0.0-alpha.1..<41.2.1` / `42.0.0-alpha.1..<42.0.0-beta.3`, fixed in **39.8.10 / 40.9.0 / 41.2.1 / 42.0.0-beta.3**, GHSA-pfmc-3mgc-p6fp, fix commit `2c24640`. The **main process** fails to check that the byte count implied by GPU-supplied frame geometry fits the received shared-memory buffer, so a compromised GPU process makes it read out of bounds while producing `paint` images. Gated on `webPreferences.offscreen` and on the attacker already owning the GPU process — hence LOW — but note the **scope change (S:C)**.
2. **The `CVE-2026-34764..34781` batch is finally dated — and it is APRIL, not August.** The **Electron project's own GHSA was opened directly for the first time** (`GHSA-8x5q-pvf5-64mp`, *"Use-after-free in offscreen shared texture release() callback"*), published by **MarshallOfSound on 2026-04-03**, reported by daffainfo: **2.3 LOW**, **CWE-416**, affected `>=33.0.0-alpha.1 <39.8.5` (+ the 40/41/42 alpha lines), patched **39.8.5 / 40.8.5 / 41.1.0 / 42.0.0-alpha.5**. The `release()` callback handed to app code on a `paint` texture can outlive its backing native state; calling it afterwards dereferences freed memory **in the main process**. This corrects the 08-08 entry's implicit assumption of a single August disclosure: **the two 2026 batches are April (`34764..34781`) and August (`70597..70612`)**.

**Why this matters more than a date fix — the taxonomy grew a third boundary.** The 08-10 run predicted CVE-2026-70598 would "extend the taxonomy sideways"; grounded, it does exactly that, and CVE-2026-34764 sits beside it. The 2026 Electron advisories now split three ways, not two: **(a) contextBridge / context-isolation** (70601, 70610, 34780) — JavaScript-semantics bugs; **(b) process privilege configuration + renderer↔main IPC integrity** (34769, 34775, 34778); and **(c) NEW — GPU process → main process** (70598, 34764) — **memory-safety** bugs, both landing in the main process, both gated on `webPreferences.offscreen`. Category (c) is untouched by every tool in the ledger: Inspectron audits *configuration*, COINDEF defends *injection*, Proton fuzzes *message passing*. The thesis can use this as a scoping sentence with teeth — *Electron's attack surface is not only the renderer→main IPC channel* — and, if the scope stays JavaScript-level, as an explicit and defensible delimitation.

**Buzz to Boom promotion check (top open item) — trigger NOT met, no change.** arXiv:2607.20698 re-fetched in full. Still a **single submission entry, [v1] Wed, 22 Jul 2026 20:11:33 UTC (575 KB)**; Subjects **cs.CR** only; **no Comments / journal-ref / venue field**; DOI still the arXiv-issued `10.48550/arXiv.2607.20698` (DataCite pending). Abstract re-read verbatim, unchanged (**589 apps → 23 zero-day MPVs, 22 to OS command execution, 13 acks / 11 fixes / 11 CVEs, Vercel bounty**). Fifth consecutive identical signal (08-07 → 08-11). A dedicated `"Buzz to Boom" … accepted` search again returned nothing beyond the arXiv record and unrelated directed-fuzzing papers. **The decisive checkpoint is now tomorrow.**

- **ACSAC '26** — confirmed unchanged from the CfP page: submission deadline **May 26**, author response **Aug 18–25**, **notification Sept 8**. Nothing can appear before then. PC chairs Nick Nikiforakis (Stony Brook) / Konrad Rieck (BIFOLD & TU Berlin); Los Angeles, Dec 7–11.
- **AsiaCCS '26** — accepted lists (both cycles, released Apr 26) and the ACM DL proceedings (`10.1145/3779208`) re-checked via search; no in-scope hits beyond the already-excluded **"Original Sin of npm"**. Conference already took place (Bangalore, Jun 1–6).
- **IEEE S&P '26 / ESORICS '26 / DSN '26 / NDSS '26** — re-checked via the standing queries; no new in-scope hits beyond what is ledgered. **S&P '27** and **NDSS '27** lists do not exist yet.

**NDSS '26 Q–Z coverage — the 08-08 action item was attempted and is still NOT closed.** Two new routes were tried today, both fail:
1. The accepted-papers fetch was re-run and **the truncation boundary is byte-exact and reproducible: 100,321 chars / 823 lines, ending mid-affiliation inside "Pruning the Tree: Rethinking RPKI Architecture from the Ground up" (P)**. Confirmed by reading the file tail directly. Q–Z is unreachable by this route, permanently.
2. **New route tried: the DBLP TOC** (`dblp.org/db/conf/ndss/ndss2026.html`, 80,287 chars / 6 lines) — fetched and grepped across the full in-scope vocabulary. It yields only two in-vocabulary entries, **both already ledgered**: `Bullseye` (in_scope) and `Cross-Boundary Mobile Tracking` (excluded). **But this TOC is also incomplete** — a targeted grep for `TranSPArent` / `Diwangkara` / `Yinzhi Cao` returns **zero occurrences**, even though TranSPArent is a confirmed NDSS '26 paper (excluded 08-07). Every substantive hit in the DBLP file falls at or before "L", so **DBLP truncates at roughly the same place and is not a workaround either.** Note also that DBLP renders as **6 enormous lines**, so it can only be searched with `-o`-style regex greps, never read linearly.
**Conclusion to carry forward:** NDSS '26 Q–Z can only be covered by **per-paper page fetches or the browser tools**. Both known Q–Z near-misses (TranSPArent, and the S–T range generally) are already excluded, so the *practical* risk is low, but coverage of that range remains unproven after three attempts by three different routes.

**Process note.** Today confirms the file-and-grep pattern as the right default for the two big listing pages: `web_fetch` saves oversized HTML to disk, and grepping that file with `-o` recovers content that linear reads silently truncate away — this is how the USENIX page was swept end-to-end today for the first time. It does **not** rescue NDSS or DBLP, because those pages are truncated **at fetch time**, not at read time; the distinction is worth remembering. Otherwise the calendar, not the search, is the story: **Aug 12 (USENIX Cycle 2)** and **Aug 13 (RAID camera-ready)** are back-to-back, and ACSAC follows on **Sept 8** — so the next three runs are the ones that matter this month. No non-academic material worth ledgering surfaced today; the Electron advisory index added on 08-08 was not re-checked this run (recommended as a standing source there — it should be re-swept on the next run that has spare budget). Scoped commit run via `scripts/commit_and_push.sh`.

## 2026-08-08 — Daily watch

**No new in-scope papers — but the run's real yield is non-academic and unusually strong: the Electron project's own 2026 advisory record, now read end-to-end from an authoritative index, and it lands directly on this thesis's boundaries.** Ledger now: **15 in-scope / 21 excluded / 8 context** (was 15/20/7). No `papers/` note written.

- **Electron upstream security advisories, 2026** → added to `context_non_venue[]`. The GitLab Advisory Database `npm/electron` index lists **35 advisories dated 2026, against 1 in 2025, 2 in 2024 and 5 in 2023** — a step change produced by two coordinated batch disclosures (`CVE-2026-34764..34781` and `CVE-2026-70597..70612`). What matters is not the count but *where they sit*. Three are **context-isolation bypasses on the contextBridge boundary**: **CVE-2026-70601** (7.5 HIGH, CWE-693, published Aug 5) — apps exposing **Promise-returning functions via `contextBridge`, i.e. the standard wrapper around `ipcRenderer.invoke`**, let untrusted web content hijack `Function.prototype.bind` and reach the isolated preload world and every capability it holds, escalating to **Node.js access** where the renderer is unsandboxed or `nodeIntegration` is on; **CVE-2026-70610** (5.4, CWE-1321) — the contextBridge object-copy path used `v8::Object::Set` ([[Set]] semantics) in `CreateProxyForAPI`, so a crafted `__proto__` fired the inherited `Object.prototype` setter and **polluted the prototype of objects landing in the privileged preload context** (fixed by switching to `CreateDataProperty`, PR #51083, GHSA-ff2p-hmqr-hxm4); **CVE-2026-34780** (8.3 HIGH) — the same bypass via bridged **WebCodecs `VideoFrame`** objects. Three more hit process/privilege configuration and IPC integrity: **CVE-2026-34769** (7.7 HIGH, an *undocumented* `commandLineSwitches` webPreference that can **disable renderer sandboxing** when `webPreferences` is built by spreading untrusted config), **CVE-2026-34775** (6.8, `nodeIntegrationInWorker` not correctly scoped in shared renderer processes — workers in frames set to `false` still got Node.js), and **CVE-2026-34778** (5.9, a **service worker spoofing reply messages on the internal IPC channel** behind `webContents.executeJavaScript()`).
- **"From Obfuscated to Obvious: A Comprehensive JavaScript Deobfuscation Tool for Security Analysis"** (Zhou, Ying, Chai, Wang — BUPT + QI-ANXIN; **NDSS 2026**) → **excluded**. Newly surfaced on the NDSS '26 accepted list; no prior sweep had ledgered it. **JSIMPLIFIER**: preprocessing → AST static analysis → dynamic execution tracing → **LLM-enhanced identifier renaming**, with multi-dimensional metrics (control/data flow, simplification, entropy, LLM readability). Releases the largest real-world obfuscated-JS dataset to date — **44,421 samples (23,212 wild malicious + 21,209 benign)** — and reports **100% processing capability across 20 obfuscation techniques**, **100% correctness on evaluation subsets**, **88.2% complexity reduction**, **>4× readability improvement**. Matched only generic `javascript` vocabulary; target is web/malware JS analyzed for readability, no desktop privilege surface → out of scope. **Kept as a methodological citation, though:** packaged Electron apps ship bundled/minified JS inside `app.asar`, so any static or taint analysis of a *shipped* app hits exactly this preprocessing problem — JSIMPLIFIER is an off-the-shelf front-end for that step and its dataset is a ready benchmark.

**Why the CVE batch is worth more than a typical context entry.** Two reasons. First, **CVE-2026-70610 is prototype pollution crossing the context-isolation boundary** — the missing link between the ledger's entire Node.js prototype-pollution line (Silent Spring, GHunter, Bullseye, UoP) and Electron proper. Those papers study the primitive in the Node.js runtime; this is the same primitive landing in Electron's own C++ bridge. The thesis can now argue the connection with a concrete, patched, CVE-numbered instance instead of by analogy. Second, **CVE-2026-70601 is a near-repeat of CVE-2023-29198** (also a contextBridge context-isolation bypass, also 2023, also "an API exposed via contextBridge can return an object"). A framework re-breaking the *same* boundary three years later, in the *standard* `ipcRenderer.invoke` wrapper pattern, is the strongest available evidence that this attack surface is not adequately covered by existing tooling — Inspectron audits *configuration*, COINDEF defends *injection*, and neither would have caught either bug. That is a motivation paragraph and a gap statement in one.

**Open item from 08-07 closed.** That run flagged a search-summary claim about CVE-2026-70601 as *asserted but unverified* and correctly refused to ledger it. It is now **confirmed** against the GitLab Advisory Database entry (CVSS 7.5, CWE-693, affected < 39.8.9 / < 40.9.2 / < 41.2.2 / < 42.0.0-beta.5, fixed Aug 5 2026, GHSA-h7rp-cf8h-j98x + NVD references). Grounding caveats recorded in the ledger: the CVE-2026-70610 root-cause and patch-diff narrative comes from an **AI-generated third-party write-up**, so the GHSA and PR #51083 must be opened directly before the C++ diff is quoted, and the publication date of the `34764..34781` batch was **not** confirmed this run.

**Buzz to Boom promotion check (top open item) — trigger NOT met, no change.** arXiv:2607.20698 re-fetched: still a **single submission entry, [v1] Wed, 22 Jul 2026 20:11:33 UTC (575 KB)**, Subjects **cs.CR**, **no Comments / journal-ref / venue tag**, DOI still the arXiv-issued `10.48550/arXiv.2607.20698` (DataCite pending). **Yinzhi Cao's homepage was re-fetched and grepped: "Buzz to Boom" still does not appear anywhere on it** — the only "Electron" string on the page is his Tsinghua *Electronic Engineering* B.E. Same signal as 08-07, now a two-run pattern. Stays in `context_non_venue[]`. Landing spots, nearest first: **USENIX Sec '26 Cycle 2 (embargo lifts Aug 12 — 4 days out)**, **CCS '26**, **NDSS '27**, **S&P '27**.

Per-venue status this run:
- **NDSS '26** — one new exclusion (above). **The truncation boundary was re-measured and is confirmed structural:** the accepted-papers fetch stops at **823 lines / ~100 kB, ending inside "Pruning the Tree" (P)**, so **Q–Z is unreachable in a single fetch** — exactly the gap that hid TranSPArent and DOM-XSS from earlier sweeps. **New this run: the `/ndss-program/symposium-2026/` page was tried as an alternate route and truncates too** (979 lines / ~118 kB, ending at "RoundRole", also **R**), so it is *not* a workaround. Domain-restricted searches over `ndss-symposium.org` for the in-scope vocabulary returned no Q–Z hits. **Action for next run: NDSS '26 Q–Z still needs either per-paper page fetches or the browser tools; the two known Q–Z near-misses are already excluded, but coverage of that range remains unproven.**

Full in-scope vocabulary grep across those 56: the only hits were **SMASH** (matched "hybrid" — hybrid MPC, false positive) and the already-excluded **"Cutting the Gordian Knot"** (PyPI). **`/cycle2-accepted-papers` does not yet exist** (blocked as non-provenance, and no such link is exposed). **Aug 12 remains the single highest-value checkpoint on the calendar** and is now 4 days out.
- **CCS '26** — accepted list **still not public**. `sigsac.org/ccs/CCS2026/` re-fetched in full: "Latest News" top item is **still July 13** (registrations open), no accepted-papers link in the nav. Unchanged since 08-01. The Hague, Nov 15–19.
- **RAID '26** — accepted list **still empty**, now **~4.5 weeks** after the Jul 10 notification. Both `raid2026.org/` and `/accepted.html` re-fetched: the accepted page renders the "Accepted papers" heading followed by nothing, and the homepage Updates feed still tops out at **Jul 27** (registration). **Camera-ready is Aug 13 — five days out and the most likely trigger for population.** Lancaster, Oct 11–14.

Worth flagging for a future run: **CVE-2026-34765** (named `window.open` targets not scoped to the opener's browsing-context group, letting a renderer navigate another renderer's child window and inherit its more permissive `webPreferences`) is a cross-window privilege-inheritance bug that no ledgered paper covers. ## 2026-08-07 — Daily watch

**No new in-scope papers — but FOUR keyword near-misses at watched venues were triaged into `excluded[]`, three of them papers this watch had never surfaced before.** Ledger now: **15 in-scope / 20 excluded / 7 context** (was 15/16/7). No `papers/` note written. This is the largest single-run addition to `excluded[]` so far, and it closes a real gap: **two NDSS '26 papers had been missed by every prior NDSS '26 sweep.**

- **"TranSPArent: Taint-style Vulnerability Detection in Generic Single Page Applications through Automated Framework Abstraction"** (Senapati Diwangkara, **Yinzhi Cao** — JHU; **NDSS 2026**) → **excluded**. Automatically abstracts SPA frameworks (Vue, React, Angular) with combined static+dynamic analysis to *recover* framework-specific taint sinks instead of hard-coding them, then runs end-to-end static dataflow detection. Grounded numbers from the NDSS abstract: **19 intermediate SPA sinks** recovered across the three frameworks, **14 of them not in the CodeQL standard library**; **11 zero-day vulnerabilities** in GitHub repositories including one with **24k+ stargazers / 30M requests per month**; **4 fixed or acknowledged**. Matched `dom-based`/taint vocabulary but the sinks are insecure **DOM APIs inside the browser sandbox** — worst case is web XSS → EXCLUDE "pure web-app XSS with no desktop/Electron angle". Worth keeping in view for one reason: it is the **same senior author as Buzz to Boom**, and its core move — *derive the sink list automatically instead of curating it* — is the obvious methodological analogue for abstracting Electron's `contextBridge`/IPC surface rather than hand-listing `child_process.exec` and friends.
- **"DOM-XSS Detection via Webpage Interaction Fuzzing and URL Component Synthesis"** (Nuno Sabino, **Darion Cassel**, Rui Abreu, Pedro Adão, Lujo Bauer, **Limin Jia** — CMU / IST Lisboa; **NDSS 2026**) → **excluded**. Presents **SWIPE**: fuzzes *user interactions* to trigger event handlers that prior static crawls never reach, plus **dynamic symbolic execution** to synthesize GET parameters and fragments that unlock more code paths. Run over **44,480 URLs** from the **Tranco top 30,000** domains: **15% more vulnerabilities** than prior work, and DSE-synthesized URL components trigger **20 new vulnerabilities**. Pure in-browser DOM-XSS, no desktop app and no Node.js privilege surface → same EXCLUDE category. **Lineage note that matters:** Cassel, Sabino and Jia are the **NodeMedic-FINE (NDSS '25)** team, already `in_scope` — so CMU's line now spans Node.js package exploit synthesis *and* browser DOM-XSS, with the Electron middle ground (renderer DOM → IPC → main-process sink) untouched by either. That is a gap statement the thesis can use directly.
- **"The First Large-Scale Systematic Study of Python Class Pollution Vulnerability"** (Zhengyu Liu, Jiacheng Zhong, **Jianjia Yu**, Muxi Lyu, Zifeng Kang, **Yinzhi Cao** — JHU; **IEEE S&P 2026**) → **excluded**. **Pyrl**, a static *operational* taint analysis over "get"/"set" primitives producing the first taxonomy of class pollution (**2 gets × 3 sets → 6 vulnerability types**); applied to **>500,000 Python programs** from GitHub and PyPI → **47 zero-day exploitable class pollutions**, including **Microsoft Azure CLI** and **Google Mesop** (both acknowledged and patched), **5 developer fixes, 7 CVEs**. Grounded on the author-hosted PDF's abstract + §1 opening. Matched `prototype pollution` as its **Python analogue**, but there is no JavaScript prototype chain, no Node.js runtime and no desktop angle → outside the INCLUDE scope, same rationale as the other PyPI exclusions. Citable only as a cross-language framing point: pollution-class bugs are a property of dynamic-language reflection, not of JS prototypes specifically.
- **"Kintsugi: Empowering LLMs to Mitigate Web Vulnerabilities via Runtime Policy Injection"** (Peng Yihao et al., Tsinghua; **USENIX Security 2026**) → **excluded**. This one was reviewed and *deliberately left unledgered* on **08-04 and 08-06** because no abstract had been published. **That changed today** — see the USENIX note below — so it is now decidable and recorded permanently. Differential syscall analysis on normal vs. malicious requests localizes vulnerability-related functions; an LLM delineates the offending snippet and sets policy trigger points; a least-privilege syscall whitelist is enforced via **eBPF + cgroups**, activated only when execution enters the protected snippet. **27 real-world CVEs across PHP, Python and Java**; **7.6 min** average response time, **9.2%** latency overhead on targeted APIs, **2.21%** throughput drop on concurrent traffic. Matched only the generic `remote code execution` keyword; server-side web-app defense → EXCLUDE.

**Why four exclusions and zero additions is the right outcome.** Every one of these was pulled in by a *config keyword* (`dom-based`, `xss`, `prototype pollution`, `remote code execution`) and every one dies on the same test: **is there a privilege boundary past the browser sandbox?** That test is now doing real work — the exclusion list has become the negative definition of the thesis scope, and three of today's four come from **exactly the two research groups (JHU/Cao, CMU/Jia) whose in-scope papers already anchor the Related Work chapter**. The useful reading is not "nothing happened" but: *both groups are actively publishing taint/fuzzing work on either side of Electron without publishing on Electron itself* — Cao's Electron paper is still an unrefereed preprint, and CMU stops at the Node.js package boundary.

**Buzz to Boom promotion check (top open item) — trigger NOT met, and today's evidence is stronger than a re-fetch.** arXiv:2607.20698 is still **[v1] Wed, 22 Jul 2026, cs.CR, no Comments / journal-ref / venue tag**, DOI still the arXiv-issued `10.48550/arXiv.2607.20698`. New this run: **Yinzhi Cao's own homepage publication list was fetched and grepped, and it does not list Buzz to Boom at all** — while its **2026 section is demonstrably current** (nine S&P '26 / NDSS '26 / ICSE '26 / ACL '26 entries, plus SRDS '26 and ICICS '26 marked "to appear"). An author who tags "to appear" entries and omits this one is the clearest available signal that **no acceptance exists yet**. Also noted: first author **Jianjia Yu graduated JHU 05/2026 (first job: Meta)**, which mildly lowers the odds of a fast resubmission cycle. Stays in `context_non_venue[]`. Landing spots, nearest first: **USENIX Sec '26 Cycle 2 (embargo lifts Aug 12 — 5 days out)**, **CCS '26**, **NDSS '27**, **S&P '27**.

Per-venue status this run:
- **NDSS '26** — **the productive venue today.** Accepted list re-fetched and re-grepped; the fetch **truncates at ~100 kB / 823 lines**, which on this alphabetical list reaches only into the **"P"** entries, so `TranSPArent` (T) is **beyond the truncation and can only be reached by direct paper-page fetch** — which is exactly why two earlier sweeps missed it. Both new exclusions above were confirmed by fetching their individual NDSS paper pages (full abstracts). **Action for next run: NDSS '26 must be swept in alphabet ranges or via per-paper pages; a single fetch silently stops around P.** The list totals **265 papers (113 summer + 152 fall cycle)**.

## 2026-08-06 — Daily watch

**No new in-scope papers. The backfill sweep surfaced 2 keyword near-misses at watched venues, both triaged into `excluded[]` so they never re-surface.** Ledger now: **15 in-scope / 16 excluded / 7 context** (was 15/14/7). No `papers/` note written.

- **"DoubleX: Statically Detecting Vulnerable Data Flows in Browser Extensions at Scale"** (Fass, Somé, Backes, Stock — CISPA; **ACM CCS 2021**, DOI 10.1145/3460120.3484745) → **excluded**. Surfaced from the 2020–2024 backfill as a methodological neighbour of the in-scope UntrustIDE: it defines an **Extension Dependence Graph** (control/data flow + pointer analysis + explicit modelling of message passing within and outside an extension) and runs it over **154,484 Chrome extensions**, flagging **278** with suspicious data flows — **89%** of those flows externally influenceable, **~93%** detection on a labelled vulnerable set. Excluded because the target is the **Chrome extension platform, which is sandboxed and has no Node.js/desktop privilege surface** → EXCLUDE "generic Chromium browser bugs unrelated to embedded/desktop apps". Worth stating the contrast explicitly, because it is the sharpest one in the ledger: **UntrustIDE's entire premise is that VS Code (Electron) extensions are *not* sandboxed and run with full app privileges**, whereas DoubleX's worst case is UXSS / data exfiltration inside the browser. Still citable as a *methodological* reference for message-flow-aware static analysis of extension code.
- **"Open Access Alert: Studying the Privacy Risks in Android WebView's Web Permission Enforcement"** (Trung Tin Nguyen, Ben Stock — CISPA; **AsiaCCS 2025**, Cycle 1, DOI 10.1145/3708821.3710821) → **excluded**. First large-scale study of web-permission enforcement in WebView; automated pipeline flagged **12,109 potentially privacy-harmful Android apps** that render websites in a WebView without re-implementing the per-site permission prompts a browser would give. Matched the config keyword **`webview`** but is explicitly **Android/mobile** → EXCLUDE "mobile-only studies", same rationale as the already-excluded "Identity Confusion in WebView-based Mobile App-in-app Ecosystems" (USENIX Sec '22) and "Cross-Boundary Mobile Tracking" (NDSS '26). Kept as an analogy citation only: the *pattern* it documents — an embedded web engine whose host app must re-implement browser security guarantees and routinely fails to — is precisely the Electron argument, on a different platform.

## 2026-08-05 — Daily watch

**No new in-scope papers at the 9 watched venues — but the unfiltered backfill sweep surfaced TWO directly on-topic master's theses, both added to `context_non_venue[]`.** Ledger now: **15 in-scope / 14 excluded / 7 context** (was 15/14/5). No `papers/` note written (context items don't get one).

- **"An Analysis of the State of Electron Security in the Wild"** (Benjamin Altpeter, **TU Braunschweig thesis, Aug 2020**, Zenodo DOI 10.5281/zenodo.5910501, CC-BY-4.0). Topically **PRIMARY**, venue-wise out of scope. This is the *earliest large-scale "state of Electron security in the wild" measurement the watch has found* — **1,204 open- and closed-source Electron applications** analyzed for security indicators (Electron security features enabled/disabled, use of dangerous functions), preceded by a survey of known Electron attack classes contrasting their impact against the same attacks inside a browser sandbox. Abstract's own conclusion: awareness and secure defaults are improving, but *"most apps don't take advantage of Electron's security features and use of dangerous functions is common."* Value to the thesis: a **2020 baseline** to compare against Inspectron's 2024 numbers (the misconfiguration-prevalence question over time), and prior art that a master's thesis on exactly this topic is an established format. **Grounding: Zenodo abstract only — the 473.7 kB PDF was not read**, so no number beyond "1,204 apps" should be quoted without opening it.
- **"Differential Security Analysis of Cross-Platform Electron Applications"** (Claudio Paloscia, **MS thesis, University of Illinois Chicago, May 2025**; advisor **Jason Polakis**, committee **Chris Kanich** + Stefano Zanero). Topically **PRIMARY**, venue-wise out of scope — and the more interesting of the two. **This is the same lab as Inspectron** (Ali, Ghasemisharif, Kanich, Polakis — USENIX Sec '24, already `in_scope`), i.e. the UIC line's follow-on work. It takes a **differential-testing** angle this watch has not seen at any watched venue: compare Electron's implementation of web security mechanisms **against Google Chrome's**, via manual testing plus the **Web Platform Tests (WPT)** cross-browser suite. Reported divergences: **CORS, permissions, cookie handling, and above all the notion of an *origin* for local (`file://`) HTML content and webviews**, which "substantially differs from that of modern browsers" — consequence claimed in the abstract is that third-party web resources can **navigate the file system and reach sensitive information**; concludes by advocating stricter Electron security defaults. **Grounding: UIC INDIGO record metadata + abstract only; PDF not read.**

**Why these two matter more than a typical context entry.** They fill the one methodological slot the in-scope set does not cover. The ledger already has config-auditing (Inspectron), DOM-type enforcement (NDSS'23), injection defense (COINDEF), directed fuzzing (Proton, still arXiv-only) and the whole Node.js-runtime line (ODGEN → UoP → Silent Spring → GHunter → NodeMedic-FINE). **Differential testing of Electron-vs-Chrome web-platform semantics is absent from all of them** — and it is a discovery technique a master's thesis can realistically execute, since WPT is off-the-shelf. A **promotion trigger** is recorded on the Paloscia entry: if Polakis's group publishes this at a watched venue (a plausible Inspectron follow-up), promote it to `in_scope` PRIMARY with a full note. Both entries record explicitly that grounding is abstract-only.

## 2026-08-04 — Daily watch

**ONE NEW IN-SCOPE PAPER — and it is the most thesis-central *published* paper the watch has found since Inspectron.** Ledger now: **15 in-scope / 14 excluded / 5 context** (was 14/14/5).

- **`ndss2024-untrustide` — "UntrustIDE: Exploiting Weaknesses in VS Code Extensions"** (Lin, Koishybayev, Dunlap, Enck, Kapravelos — NC State; **NDSS 2024**, DOI 10.14722/ndss.2024.24073, **Distinguished Paper Award**). **PRIMARY.** Why it belongs: the config's very first INCLUDE line is "Electron apps (**VS Code**, Slack, Teams, Discord, etc.) security/vulnerabilities," and this is the ecosystem-scale vulnerability-DISCOVERY study of exactly that app. The paper states in §I/§II that VS Code is built on Electron (Chromium + Node.js) and that extensions, **unlike browser extensions, are not sandboxed** — they run with the full privileges of the VS Code application (shell, filesystem, network, listening servers). Grounded numbers from the NDSS open-access camera-ready: marketplace scraped **Jan 2023**, **43,436** extensions → **25,402** containing code; **12 custom CodeQL taint rules** encoding **4 sources** (workspace settings, workspace file read, network response, locally spawned web server) × **3 sinks** (shell command, `eval()`, file write); **21 extensions with verified code-injection PoCs totalling >6M installations** — 7 (settings→shell, >5.7M installs, incl. **git-graph** at 5.1M via `git.path` → `child_process.spawn`), 6 (settings→`eval`, >80k, incl. **scss-lint**), 4 (file→shell, >121k, incl. **CMake Test Explorer**), 4 (file→`eval`, >11k, incl. **autodocblocker**), plus **1 partial file-integrity** attack via a localhost Express endpoint (**Azure Data Lake Tools**, 279k+). Supply-chain context: of the 15,185 extensions with lock files, **13,655 have >100 transitive deps** and **9,710 (63.9%) import a package with a critical npm advisory**; **5 extensions call Heroku URLs, 4 of them pointing at deleted apps** whose subdomains are re-registrable. Disclosure: 6 developers confirmed, 3 shipped fixes; GitHub Security + Microsoft VS Code team notified.

**Why this changes the Related Work framing.** Inspectron (USENIX'24) counts *misconfigurations* and explicitly does not prove exploitability; UntrustIDE delivers *verified exploits* on a real Electron app but stops at the **extension-host (Node.js) boundary** — it never touches `nodeIntegration`, `contextIsolation`, preload/`contextBridge`, or renderer↔main IPC. The note records this explicitly: the PRIMARY tag reflects the *target* (a real Electron desktop app) and the *task* (discovery with PoCs), while the Electron-framework framing in §5 is the analyst's. That leaves the thesis a clean two-axis gap to claim: **single app → many Electron apps**, and **extension host → process boundary (IPC/preload)**. Also a lineage note: **Koishybayev & Kapravelos are the authors of Mininode (RAID'20)**, already in the ledger — NCSU's Node.js attack-surface line extends into the IDE/Electron app layer here.

**How it was found (repeat of yesterday's process lesson).** UntrustIDE surfaced as a *related-work mention* inside an unfiltered backfill standing query ("Electron desktop app security … 2021–2025"), not from any accepted list — the same failure mode as the 08-03 additions: ~60 prior runs concentrated on newly-posted 2026 material and missed a Distinguished Paper at a watched venue from 2024. The unfiltered backfill sweep is now clearly earning its keep and should stay in every run until the 2020–2024 window is exhausted.

Across the in-scope vocabulary (electron / node.js / npm / webview / renderer / preload / contextBridge / IPC / prototype pollution / desktop / chromium / CEF / supply chain) the Day-1 hits were: **"The Art of Hide and Seek: Making Pickle-Based Model Supply Chain Poisoning Stealthy Again"** (Python pickle / ML model supply chain) and **"Anchors that Don't Lift: … Supply Chain Driven Kernel Lock-In … in SOHO Devices"** (embedded firmware) — both matched only the generic "supply chain" term, neither npm/Node.js/Electron, so **not ledgered** (consistent with prior handling of non-keyword near-misses). The two literal "Electron" string hits on the conference page were **false positives** (University of Electronic Science and Technology; Electronic Frontier Foundation — PC/committee lists). One title reviewed and deliberately not ledgered: **"Kintsugi: Empowering LLMs to Mitigate Web Vulnerabilities via Runtime Policy Injection"** (Tsinghua) — no abstract published yet, no config keyword hit, reads as pure web-vulnerability mitigation. **Action for the next run: re-fetch Technical Sessions and confirm Thursday Aug 13 / Friday Aug 14 sessions, which are where the embargoed Cycle-2 papers ("Paper Title Under Embargo" placeholders are already visible on the site) will appear.**

## 2026-08-03 — Daily watch

**TWO NEW IN-SCOPE PAPERS ADDED — the first `in_scope[]` additions since 2026-06-05.** Both are *backfill* finds at watched venues (the config's `backfill_from_year` is 2020), surfaced by the prototype-pollution / Node.js standing query rather than by any newly-published accepted list. Ledger now: **14 in-scope / 14 excluded / 5 context** (was 12/14/5).

- **`usenixsec2022-odgen` — "Mining Node.js Vulnerabilities via Object Dependence Graph and Query"** (Li, Kang, Hou, Cao — Johns Hopkins; **USENIX Security 2022**, pp. 143–160). **ADJACENT.** Why it belongs: the foundational *general-purpose* vulnerability-mining framework for the Node.js runtime that Electron's main process embeds — it introduces the **Object Dependence Graph (ODG)**, which makes JavaScript objects and prototype-chain lookups first-class queryable entities where CPG (used for C/C++ and PHP) cannot. Grounded numbers: models **13 of 16** CVE categories (**302/330** vulns, Jan 2019–Sept 2020); on **300K NPM packages** (crawled 2020-02-25) reported **2,964**, manually checked **264** → **180 TP / 84 FP**, **70 CVEs** (app-level 43, package-level 137); FP rate **32%** vs. JSJoern **75%** and JSTap-vul **80%** (baselines lack points-to info). `deparam` → PDX-Parks case study shows package→application escalation. Note written from the **USENIX open-access camera-ready**. This is the methodological ancestor of every later Node.js/Electron discovery tool and the head of the JHU/Cao lineage (**ODGEN '22 → UoP '24 → Buzz to Boom '26**).
- **`sp2024-uop` — "Undefined-oriented Programming: Detecting and Chaining Prototype Pollution Gadgets in Node.js Template Engines for Malicious Consequences"** (Liu, An, Cao — Johns Hopkins; **IEEE S&P 2024**, Xplore doc 10646867). **ADJACENT.** Why it belongs: the *second-stage* counterpart to pollution detection — the first framework to detect **chained** gadgets (not just direct ones), via concolic execution with **undefined properties as symbols**, which is exactly the escalation step that turns a renderer-side pollution primitive into ACE once direct gadgets are patched. Grounded numbers: **25 zero-day gadgets, 13 chained, 5 already fixed** (abstract); on 40 `consolidate.js` template engines (latest versions Feb 2023) UOPF **TP 26 / FN 0 / FP 0 / FPR 0** vs. Silent Spring **TP 1 / FN 25 / FPR 0.8** and SS-UoPF-init **TP 2 / FN 24 / FPR 0.6**; legacy set UOPF FNR 0.333 / FPR 0 vs. SS 0.943 / 0.833. Zero FPs because every gadget is auto-verified with a synthesized exploit. First taxonomy of chained gadgets (vertical/horizontal × control-/data-flow dependent) and first curated template-engine gadget dataset (15 gadgets, 13 engines). Affected engines include node-blade, ejs, squirrellyJS, dustjs, ect/coffee-script, doT, pug, jade, hamlet, mote, ractive.js, saker. Note written from the **author-hosted PDF** (`yinzhicao.org/UoP/UoP-Oakland.pdf`). **Flagged caveat:** abstract (25/13), §5.2 (21 reported / 20 unique), Table 2 (24 rows) and Table 3 (26 TP) do not reconcile in the read text — cite the abstract figures and re-check the Xplore camera-ready before quoting the others.

Both notes explicitly record that **neither paper mentions Electron at all** (no renderer/main, preload, contextBridge or IPC content) — the ADJACENT tag and all Electron framing are the analyst's, consistent with how Silent Spring / GHunter / Mininode / HODOR were handled. Both notes close §5 with the same gap statement that positions the thesis: these tools cover the **Node.js runtime inside one process**, leaving the **framework and process-boundary** surface (Electron packaging, renderer↔main IPC, preload/contextBridge, attacker-reachable UI entry points) open.

**Buzz to Boom promotion check (top open item) — trigger NOT met, no change.** Re-fetched the arXiv abstract page for **arXiv:2607.20698** (Yu, Liu, Li, Sun, Cao — Johns Hopkins): submission history still shows a single entry — **[v1] Wed, 22 Jul 2026 20:11:33 UTC (575 KB)** — Subjects **cs.CR**, **no Comments field, no journal-ref, no venue tag** → still **no confirmed acceptance** at any of the 9 watched venues. (Today's two additions are the same lab's earlier work, which strengthens the lineage framing for that eventual note.)

## 2026-07-26 — Daily watch

**+1 context (no new watched-venue paper).** Swept all 9 venues (USENIX Sec, IEEE S&P, NDSS, CCS, ACSAC, RAID, ESORICS, AsiaCCS, DSN) via web search across the standing queries (Electron app security, nodeIntegration/contextIsolation, preload/contextBridge, renderer↔main IPC, CEF/WebView, XSS→RCE, prototype pollution, npm/supply-chain), focused on 2026 and newly posted material.

**One genuinely new, strongly on-topic candidate surfaced and was triaged into `context_non_venue` (NOT `in_scope`, because it is arXiv-only):** **"Buzz to Boom: Detecting Message Progression Vulnerabilities in Electron Applications via Segmented Directed Fuzzing"** (Jianjia Yu, Zhengyu Liu, Ziyang Li, Yu Sun, Yinzhi Cao — Johns Hopkins; **arXiv:2607.20698**, v1 submitted **22 Jul 2026**, cs.CR). This is the **most thesis-central out-of-venue item this watch has ever surfaced** — a *direct Electron vulnerability-DISCOVERY* system, not merely Node.js-runtime-adjacent. It defines a new bug class, **Message Progression Vulnerabilities (MPVs)** — attacker-controlled inputs that chain across processes via message passing to reach privileged/terminal sinks (OS command execution) — and presents **Proton**, a *segmented directed fuzzing* framework: it decomposes end-to-end fuzzing into per-process segments along message-passing boundaries (each segment either reaches a sink or propagates the payload to seed the next segment's corpus), uses **LLM-driven agentic static analysis** to identify source→sink paths / segment boundaries and to synthesize per-segment harnesses + seeds, then composes and validates an end-to-end exploit PoC. Grounded numbers (arXiv abstract verbatim + full-text intro/contributions, grepped): **589 real Electron apps → 23 zero-day MPVs, 22 to full RCE / OS command execution** (projects >50k GitHub stars); **13 acknowledgments, 11 fixes, 11 CVEs**, plus a **Vercel bug bounty on Hyper**; **17 more** zero-days than non-segmented end-to-end fuzzing. It explicitly positions past DOM-tree type (NDSS '23) / Inspectron (USENIX '24) / COINDEF (S&P '25) as unable to detect these cross-process, multi-step exploits.

**Why context, not in_scope:** the arXiv abstract page carries **no Comments/venue field** and the full text carries **no venue annotation** → **no confirmed acceptance at any of the 9 watched venues.** Per the ledger's consistent out-of-venue precedent (PoCGen, the UIC master's thesis, PoPETs '25, JavaSith, "Developers Are Victims Too", the LLM-taint preprints), arXiv-only work is **not** added to `in_scope[]` or `excluded[]`. It is recorded in `context_non_venue[]` (and detailed in `papers/_context-non-venue.md`) as the **top promotion candidate** — promote to `in_scope` PRIMARY with a full `papers/` note the moment a watched venue publishes it (**watch CCS '26** — notifications Jul 17; **USENIX Sec '26 Cycle 2** — release Aug 12; **NDSS '27**; **S&P '27**). Given the timing (posted 5 days after CCS '26 notifications), CCS '26 is the most likely near-term landing spot to re-check.

Every other in-scope academic result searches returned is already in the ledger: the Electron core set (**DOM-tree type** NDSS '23, **Inspectron** USENIX Sec '24, **COINDEF** S&P '25, **XRCE/XGuard** CCS '22) and the Node.js/runtime adjacency set (**Silent Spring** USENIX Sec '23, **GHunter** USENIX Sec '24, **HODOR** CCS '23, **NodeShield** CCS '25, **Mininode** RAID '20, **Bullseye** NDSS '26, **GASKET/Best of Both Worlds** S&P '26, **NodeMedic-FINE** NDSS '25).

Per-venue status this run:
- **CCS '26** — accepted list **still not public** in search results (only the HotCRP sites `ccs2026a/b.hotcrp.com` and `sigsac.org/ccs/CCS2026/` surface; no accepted-papers URL). Unchanged from 07-25 (notifications were Jul 17). **This is the top checkpoint** given Buzz to Boom's timing. The Hague, Nov 15–19.
- **RAID '26** — accepted list **still not public** in search results (only `raid2026.org` / `raid26.hotcrp.com`); consistent with 07-22→07-25 where direct fetches showed the "Accepted papers" section rendered but empty (notifications Jul 10; Lancaster, UK, Oct 11–14).
- **USENIX Sec '26 Cycle 2** — still not browsable; Cycle-2 embargoed papers release on the symposium's first day (Baltimore, Aug 12–14, 2026). Cycle 1 already fully grepped (sole hit remains the excluded **PyGuard** / "Cutting the Gordian Knot").
- **ESORICS '26** (Rome, Sept 14–18) / **ACSAC '26** (acceptances Sept 8; Dec 7–11, LA) — accepted lists still not browsable.
- **IEEE S&P '26 / NDSS '26 / AsiaCCS '26 (both cycles) / DSN '26** — fully grepped in prior runs; re-checked via search this run, no new in-scope hits.

**Tooling note:** `web_fetch` was heavily **HTTP-429 rate-limited** for most of this run — it took several spaced retries to retrieve even the arXiv abstract page, and direct re-fetches of `sigsac.org/ccs/CCS2026/` and `raid2026.org/accepted.html` were not spent this run. The 2026 Electron surface searches returned otherwise remains **CVE disclosures and industry write-ups**, not peer-reviewed top-venue papers: the recurring **CVE-2026-34774** (offscreen-rendering UAF), **CVE-2026-34781** (`clipboard.readImage` DoS), **CVE-2026-34765** (multi-window trust-level permission inheritance → ACE), **CVE-2026-34769** (`commandLineSwitches` renderer switch injection → sandbox disable), and **CVE-2026-26462** (RCE in an offline Hospital Management System Electron app with nodeIntegration:true / contextIsolation:false), alongside the previously-noted **UIC master's thesis** "Differential Security Analysis of Cross-Platform Electron Applications" (Figshare/Indigo, out of venue). ## 2026-07-21 — Maintenance (no paper sweep)

**Infrastructure fix, not a venue sweep.** Triggered by the user asking why the automated `git push` keeps failing. Root cause confirmed: the run sandbox intermittently blocks file **deletion** (`rm` / `find -delete` → "Operation not permitted"), while git releases some of its lock files via `unlink()`. So a routine `git status`/`git add` leaves an undeletable `.git/index.lock` (plus `HEAD.lock` / ref `.lock`s) behind, and that jams every following git command. At inspection time 347 stale `*.lock*` files had accumulated and a live `.git/index.lock` (dated 04:18) was blocking the whole repo — which is why nothing reached the push step.

Fixes applied: (1) cleared the stuck `.git/index.lock` by **renaming** it aside (rename is permitted even when delete is not); (2) hardened `scripts/commit_and_push.sh` to fall back to rename-aside for any lock it cannot delete, and to run that cleanup **before every git step** rather than once at the start, so a lock left mid-run no longer blocks the next command. Verified `git ls-remote` and `git push` both reach `origin` and local == remote — auth + network are fine (the embedded credential is valid; push itself was never the failure). No ledger or `papers/` change this run.

- **NDSS 2026 — Bullseye** (Houis et al.) — dynamic detection of prototype pollution in npm packages; 290 zero-days / 149 CVEs across ~50k packages, no false positives. Discovery tool for the XSS→RCE escalation primitive Electron apps inherit.
- **ACM CCS 2023 — HODOR** (Wang et al.) — seccomp syscall-whitelisting shrinks Node.js attack surface to 19.42% avg, <3% overhead. Backfill miss; sibling to Mininode.
- **ACM CCS 2025 — NodeShield** (Cornelissen & Balliu) — runtime SBOM+capability (CBOM) enforcement; blocks 98.51% of 67 known supply-chain attacks, <1ms overhead. (Balliu also co-authored Silent Spring.)

Recorded **3 new excluded** (NDSS 2026, matched a keyword but out of scope): Cross-Boundary Mobile Tracking (mobile WebView), From Noise to Signal (generic npm SCA), FirmCross (firmware C-Lua hybrid web services).

Grounding: all 3 notes from open-access full text (Concordia author PDF for Bullseye; arXiv preprints for HODOR & NodeShield). Ledger now 9 in-scope / 8 excluded / 4 context. Note: the parent `.git` is not mounted in this sandbox, so the scoped commit must be run on the user's machine.

## 2026-06-02 — Initial backfill (2020 → present)

Swept all 9 watched venues (USENIX Security, IEEE S&P, NDSS, CCS, ACSAC, RAID, ESORICS, AsiaCCS, DSN) for 2020–2026.

Added **6 in-scope notes**:
- NDSS 2023 — DOM-tree type (Jin et al.) — PRIMARY
- CCS 2022 — XRCE / XGuard (Xiao et al.) — PRIMARY
- USENIX Security 2024 — Inspectron (Ali et al.) — PRIMARY
- IEEE S&P 2025 — COINDEF (Yang et al.) — PRIMARY
- USENIX Security 2023 — Silent Spring (Shcherbakov et al.) — ADJACENT
- RAID 2020 — Mininode (Koishybayev & Kapravelos) — ADJACENT

Recorded **5 excluded** (at a watched venue but out of scope) and **4 context** papers (field-defining work outside the 9 venues).

Key finding: the topic is concentrated at the top-4 venues; the 1.5-tier venues are effectively empty on Electron-app vulnerabilities (only Mininode at RAID, and it targets server-side Node.js).

Grounding: all notes built from full text except CCS 2022 (ACM paywalled → abstract + secondary sources, flagged in-note).
